Vv


This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India has been
prepared for submission to the Governor under Article 151 of the
Constitution for being laid before the State Legislature.

The Report, covering the year 2012-13, contains significant results of the
compliance audits and performance audits of the Departments of the
Government of Madhya Pradesh under Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector
including Departments of Co-operative, Farmer Welfare and Agriculture
Development, Forest, Narmada Valley Development, Public Works,
Panchayat and Rural Development, Rural Industries and Water Resources.
However, Departments under the General, Social and Revenue Sectors are
excluded and are covered in the Reports on the General, Social and Revenue
Sectors,

The cases mentioned in the Report are among those which came to notice in
the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2012-13 as well as those
which came to notice in earlier years but could not be reported in previous
Audit Reports; matters relating to the period subsequent to year 2012-13
have also been included, wherever necessary.

Audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards issued
by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India, based on the Auditing
Standards of the International Organisation of Supreme Audit Institutions.

This Report contains an overview of significant audit observations and three
Chapters. Chapter-1 of this Report narrates the budget profile of the State,
planning and conduct of audit, responses of the departments to the audit and
follow up of Audit Reports. Chapter-2 includes Performance Audits on
Construction of Bridges by Public Works Department, Working of Co-
operative Department, Management of Forest Offences, Rashtriya Krishi
Vikas Yojna, Quality Assurance in Construction of Rural Roads and Internal
Control in Water Resources Department. Chapter-3 deals with compliance
audit findings of various departments under Economic Sector.

vii


This Report of Comptroller and Auditor General of India on Economic (Nou-
PSUs) Sector, Government of Madhya Pradesh for the year ended 31 March
2013 includes six Performance Audits and 15 paragraphs arising from audit of
the financial transactions. Total money value of audit observations is हैं. 293.71
crore. A summary of the important findings is given below:

1.1 Construction of Bridges

The responsibility of planning, designing and construction of bridges and
railway over bridges in the Stale is with Public Works Department.
Construction of the bridge works was funded from the State Plan Budget,
National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development and Central Road
Fund. During the period 2008-13, expenditure तारे 695.50 crore was incurred
on construction of 385 bridges. A performance audit of “Construction of
Bridges” showed deficiencies in planning. execution, contract management
and monitoring of the execution of works. Important audit findings are as
under:

> There was lack of planning and investigation for bridge works. Selection
of unsuitable sites. inadequate survey and investigation delayed execution
of 20 bridges from one month to cight years and resulted increase in cost
by ₹ै 11.40 crore. Due to inappropriate drawings and designs, Tawa bridge
(cost: हैं 4.19 crore) and approaches of Kurel bridge and Sitarewa bridge
were washed away.

v

We observed deficiencies in work execution. Completion of 52 bridge
works delayed by two to 88 months due to award of works without land
acquisition, delays in construction of approaches of bridges and approval
ot drawings/designs as Well as in taking action on detaulting contractors.
This has also resulted in untiuitful expenditure and extra cost on works.

Vv

Contract management was not effective. Due to short claim of rebate and
irregular grant of time extension for delays in execution resulted in undue
financial benefit (¥ 4.47 crore) to contractors and absolved them from the
liability of penalty (@ 1.08 crore) besides payment of escalation (© 1.11
crore). Further, initial rejection of request for change the type of
foundation of a bridge and subsequent acceptance of same after
retendering, resulted in execution of the works at an extra cost of F 1.28
crore.

> Monitoring of work execution by the Department was deficient as
reflected from non conduct of inspections of works by SEs and lack of

pursuance at the higher level for acquisition of land required for works.
(Paragraph 2.1)

1.2 Working of Co-operative Department

Co-operative Department has been entrusted with the administrative
responsibility of ensuring constitution, operation, audit and inspection of
Co-operative societies and implementation of various schemes aimed at
developing different classes of the society particularly weaker sections and
women through Co-operative Societies. There are 36,062 working



Audit Report Economic Sector (Non P'SUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

Co-operative societies registered with the Department, of which nine are apex
societies. A review of the working of the Department (2012-13) revealed the
following deficiencies:

> The Department failed to show substantial progress in ensuring greater
autonomy of Co-operative sector. As a result, the State was deprived of
Central assistance of 2% 667.21 crore for revival of Co-operative

banks/societies.

» There was pendency in recovery of share capital of ¥ 38.13 crore from
Co-operative societies and Co-operative banks and recovery of loans plus
interest amounting to 223.61 crore from the societies.

» There was pendency in audit (15 to 21 per cent) of the Co-operative
societies. The Department did not take action against the societies for non-
maintenance of records and not holding Annual General Meetings and also
against the Board of Directors of Co-operative banks for fi i
recovery of loans given.

> Integrated Co-operative Development Project schemes, for economic
development in Agriculture Sector, were not completed within the
stipulated period. The Department admitted the claim of interest subsidy
on crop loans only on the basis of utilisation certificates. without obtaining
audited accounts in support of claims for interest subsidy,

> There was shortage of manpower in many cadres of the Department
adversely affecting its functioning. Also, the Department was not having
any set up for internal audit and inspection of records was not conducted
by DDOs as required in MP Treasury Code.

(Paragraph 2.2)
1.3 Management of forest offences in Madhya Pradesh

Management of forest against offences and forest protection is one of the
primary responsibilities of the State Forest Department. The management and
protection of forests is regulated mainly by the Indian Forests Act, 1927. The
Performance Audit examined adequacy and efficacy of the Forest Department
on prevention, detection, registration, investigation, disposal and monitoring
of forest offences. Important audit findings are as under:

> There was 213 per cent increase in encroachment cases during 2008-12
Fifty one per cent of wireless sets for prevention of forest offences were not
in working condition. There was shortage of 3,870 staff affecting the
prevention of forest offences.

> Detection of forest offence was deficient. Use of satellite data for detection
of offences was not adequate. Use of hired vehicles for patrolling of
sensitive area was not ensured. There was shortfall (11 to 40 per cent) in
beat inspections.

> The registration of forest offence cases was not properly monitored due to
non submission of counterfoils of Preliminary Offence Report to the
Divisions. There were delays up to five months in registration of cases.

> Investigation of forest offences was deficient. The procedure adopted for
fixing commercial rates of timber was not based on uniform system of the



Overview

classification of girth classes of timbers. The quantity of minerals extracted
through illegal mining was not reported in 1264 cases. We observed delay
in investigation and submission of cases to Courts. As a result, 5,592 forest
offence cases were time barred.
> Monitoring of forest offence cases was deficient as the inspection reports
for controlling illegal mining were not submitted to the Government. Forms
for reporting of offences did not provide important information. Besides.
timber accounts were not prepared for long period.
(Paragraph 2.3)
1.4 Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana
Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY), a Centrally Sponsored Scheme aims
at achieving four per cen/ annual growth in the agricultural sector during the
eleventh plan period, by ensuring holistic development of agriculture and
allied sectors. In the State, 402 RKVY projects were executed by Farmer
Welfare and Agriculture Development (FW&AD) Department during the
period 2007-13, of which, 297 projects were completed. Performance Audit of
implementation of RKVY during 2008-13 revealed the following deficiencies:

> The work of preparation of District Agricultural Plans (DAPs) was awarded
belatedly in June 2009. The plans prepared for the years 2007-08 to
2010-11 were of no use due to delayed preparation.

> The FW&AD and Jawahar Lal Nehru Krishi Vishwa Vidhyalaya (INK VV)
utilised %8.75 crore for purposes other than those specified in the Scheme.
Animal Husbandry Department parked unspent balance of 13.94 crore
pertaining to 2007-10 which remained unspent till the end of year 2010-11
and 21.45 crore till 2012-13 in Personal Deposit Account .

> The formation of State level monitoring cell, required for monitoring and
implementation of projects under RKVY, was still under process
(September 2013) though the projects were taken up since 2007-08.

> In JNKVV, Jabalpur three research projects were abandoned thereby
defeating the purpose of the projects

> In Custom Hiring Centers, tractors and implements were underutilized to
the extent of 20 per cent to 56 per cent of targets set. Four cattle breeding
farms did not function due to incomplete civil works. Cold chain for the
purpose of preserving potency of vaccines was not established in the
manner as planned

> Project-wise accounts/ledgers were not maintained tor accounting of
expenditure on projects. FW&AD has not formed any dedicated monitoring
cell for evaluation and monitoring of performance of projects

(Paragraph 2.4)
LS Quality assurance in construction of rural roads
A three tier quality management mechanism has been operationalised under
Pradhan Mantri Gramin Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) for ensuring that quality of
assets created conforms to the prescribed standards. A Performance Audit on

Quality Assurance in construction of rural roads by MPRRDA during the
period 2008-13 revealed various deficiencies:

xi


Audit Report Economic Sector (Non P'SUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

va There was lack of quality assurance for preparation of Detailed Project
Reports (DPRs) as reliability of data for preparation of DPRs was not
ensured. There were shortage of testing equipments in district
laboratories required for project preparation and quality control.

> Quality in respect of execution of road works was not assured as
prescribed tests for alignment, regularity and surface levels, earthwork,
gtanular work, bituminous work and concrete work were not carried
out and adequately recorded.

> Second tier quality control State Quality Monitors (SQMs) was

deficient as frequency of inspection by SQM was inadequate and PIUs
did not report compliance with their suggestions to the State Quality
Co-ordinator (SQC). There was shortfall in sites inspections by
Assistant /Executive Engineers.

(Paragraph 2.5)
1.6 Review of Internal Control in Water Resources Department

The Water Resources Department (WRD) is responsible for plannin:
estimation, execution, operation and maintenance of irrigation projects, safety
of dams, matters relating to user associations etc. There are 6109 completed
projects (13 major, 110 medium and 5986 minor) and 688 ongoing projects
(7 major, 18 medium, 663 minor) as of March 2013. A review of internal
controls in WRD during the period 2010-13 revealed deficiencies in budgetary.
financial operational and organisational controls.

> An amount of € 27.59 crore and € 162.32 crore were surrendered in the last
week of the financial year 2010-11 and 2011-12 respectively.

> Revised administrative approvals regularising the excess expenditure of
₹ 83.95 crore in respect of 48 works were not obtained as of March 2013.

> Reconciliation of cheques and remittances by the divisional officers with
the Accountant General (A&E) office delayed up to 157 months

Vv

Miscellaneous Works advance of ¥24.11 crore relating to the period trom

1957 to February 2013 against officials, contractors. suppliers and other
departments etc. were outstanding at the end of March 2013. Advances of
21257.66 crore paid to Land Acquisition Officer (LAO) for acquisition of
land for irtigation projects were directly charged to final head of
expenditure.

> Material at site accounts, contractor’s ledger and works abstract were not
maintained to ensure control over the receipt/issue of material for works
and transactions relating to advance payments, secured advances,
adjustment of advances and value of work done.

Vv

Quality control functions were inadequate as only two main laboratories
were established though it should have been established at all the 25 major
and medium projects: field laboratories were also not established at any of
the work sites.

(Paragraph 2.6)

Overview

Audit has also reported on several significant deficiencies in critical areas
which impact the effective functioning of the Government Departments/
Organisations. These are broadly categorised and grouped as:

» Non-compliance with rules

> Expenditure without propriety

> Persistent and pervasive irregularities
> Failure of oversight/governance

21 Non-Compliance with rules

For sound financial administration and financial control, it is essential that
expenditure conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the
competent authority. This not only prevents irregularities, misappropriation
and frauds, but helps in maintaining good financial discipline. This report

contains instances of non-compliance with rules involving रैं 3.49 crore. Some
significant audit findings are as under:

> Forest Department did not raise demand for revised Net Present Value
(NPV). despite orders of the Hon’ble Supreme Court and the State
Government. This resulted in short realisation of NPV amounting to
र₹ 99.81 lakh.

(Paragraph 3.1.1)
> Due to exclusion of expenditure of € 6.70 crore incurred on regeneration

for arriving at net profit, an excess payment of dividend amounting to
& 53.58 lakh was distributed by two DFOs to Joint Forest Management
Committees.

(Paragraph 3.1.2)
> The Executive Engineer (EE) WR Division Dindori made excess payment
of @ 64.26 lakh to the contractor due to payment for the quantity in excess
of 10 per cent of bill of quantity at quoted rates instead of estimated rate
plus or minus the overall percentage to the total cost of wark.

(Paragraph 3.1.3)
While according sanction of rate of an extra item (M-25 controlled
conercte) of an irrigation tank. Government (Water Resouree Department)
added tender premium on an amount of € 3,947.86 per cu m against actual
estimate of 3,118.91 per cu m. This resulted in excess payment of
₹ 44.39 lakh to the contractor

Vv

(Paragraph 3.1.4)
> While making payment for earthwork, non-deduction of cost of watering
and compaction, which were neither watered nor compacted, resulted in
acceptance of below specification earthwork valued २ 86.80 lakh leading
to unstable base of embankment, besides excess payment of F 19.29 lakh
by the EE, Mahan Canal Division, Sidhi.
(Paragraph 3.1.5)
2.2 Expenditure without propriety
Authorisation of expenditure from public funds is to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as

xiii


Audit Report Economic Sector (Non P'SUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money and
should enforce financial order and strict economy at every step. Audit has
noticed instances of impropriety, extra and infructuous expenditure, involving
% 5.92 crore are mentioned below:

> In construction of three buildings by two PWD Divisions adoption of
incorrect price of steel for calculation of price variation resulted in excess
payment of € 73.96 lakh to contractor.

(Paragraph 3.2.1)
> The Water Resources Department executed 1,17,517 cum of rock toe,
stone pitching etc. at full rate instead of labour rate only by ignoring 1.44
lakh cu m of hard rock available from carth excavation resulting in
avoidable extra expenditure of f 1.22 crore.

(Paragraph 3.2.2)
Due to adoption of incorrect price indices for material components of the
work, EE, Sanjay Sagar Project Ganjbasoda made excess payment of
45.60 lakh to the supplier on account of price variation.
(Paragraph 3.2.3)
» Inclusion in DPR and execution of item of WBM grading II below CC
pavement though not provided in the specitications resulted in extra cost
of F 3.50 crore by MPRRDA

Vv

(Paragraph 3.2.4)
2.3 Persistent and pervasive irregularities

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year बीटा year and it
becomes pervasive, when it is prevailing in the entire system. Reoccurrence of
irregularities despite being pointed out in earlier audits, is not only indicative
of non-seriousness on the part of the executives but is also an indication of
lack of effective monitoring. This, in turn, encourages willful deviations fram
observance of rules/regulations and results in weakness of the administrative
structure. Cases of persistence irregularity valuing € 5.95 crore reported in
audit are discussed below:

» The Chief Engineer, Major District Road (MDR) adopted incorrect
parameters in computation of traffic intensity, incorrect provision. and
execution of richer specification of Bituminous Macadam and Semi Dense
Bituminous Concrete instead of required Open Grade Premix Carpet with
Seal coat. This resulted in extra expenditure of हैं 3.49 crore on
construction of Major District Roads.

(Paragraph 3.3.1)
> Undue financial aid of § 2.46 crore was extended by EE, Sanjay Sagar
Project Bah river Division, Ganjbasoda (Vidisha) to the contractor due to
adoption of incorrect ratio of components in terms of value of work for
price escalation.
(Paragraph 3.3.2)
2.4. Failure of oversight/governance

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people
through fulfillment of certain goals in the area of health, education,
development and upgradation of infrastructure and public service. However,
Audit scrutiny revealed instances wherein the funds released by the
Government for creating public assets for the benefit of the community

xiv


Overview

remained unutilised/blocked and/or proved untruitful/unproductive due to
indecisiveness, lack of administralive oversight and concerted action al
various levels. Cases amounting to € 5.02 crore have been discussed below

> Survival rate of plants in compensatory afforestation remained below 30
per cent against the specified target of 75 per cent due to improper
planning and execution. This resulted in infructuous expenditure of
& 1.20 crore in two Forest Divisions, Ichhawar and Kavery of Narmada
Valley Development Department.

(Paragraph 3.4.1)
Late initiation in obtaining approval of the Forest Department led to
stoppage of a road work midway and re-tendering of the balance work.
Starting of work by EE, PWD (B&R) Satna Division without seeking
clearance from the Forest Department resulted in cost overrun of € 2.39

v

crore in upgradation work of Unchehra-Parasmaniya-Dureha road. The
works remained incomplete for 39 months.
(Paragraph 3.4.2)
>» In Harsi High Level Canal Division, Dabra, ‘Providing LDPE film below
canal lining’ was included in the estimate of a canal work, but not got
executed. This resulted in vitiating of tendering process because the lowest
tenderer would no longer have been the lowest. if the item of LDPE film,
for which lowest tenderer had quoted only हैं | per sqm against the estimate
of € 22 per sqm was excluded from the estimates.
(Paragraph 3.4.3)
» Due to improper planning by EE. WRD, Katni expenditure of € 75.34 lakh
remained untruitful on construction of Khirahni Lift Irrigation Scheme in
Katni District.
(Paragraph 3.4.4)

The audit process starts with the risk assessment of various Departments,
autonomous bodies, schemes/projects, etc. criticality/complexity of activities,
level of delegated financial powers. internal controls and concerns of
stakeholders and previous audit findings. Based on this risk assessment, the
frequency and extent of audit are decided and an Annual Audit Plan is
formulated.

Alter completion of audit, Inspection Report containing audit findings is
issued to the head of the office with a request to furnish replies within one
month. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or
further action for compliance is advised. The important audit observations
pointed out in these Inspection Reports (IRs) are processed for inclusion in the



Audit Report Economic (Non-P’SUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Audit Reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India, which are
submitted to the Governor of Madhya Pradesh under Article 151 of the
Constitution of India.

During 2012-13, audit of 468 units (compliance audit and performance audit)
of various departments/ organisations of the State was conducted by the office
of the Accountant General (Economic & Revenue Sector Audit) Madhya
Pradesh.

The Accountant General (Economic & Revenue Sectors Audit) Madhya
Pradesh conducts periodical inspection of Government Departments by test-
check of transactions and verity the maintenance of important accounting and
other records as per the prescribed rules and procedures. These inspections are
followed by the issue of Audit Inspection Reports. When important
irregularities, etc. detected during audit inspection are not settled on the spot,
these IRs are issued to the head of officers inspected, with a copy to the next
higher authorities.

The head of offices and next higher authorities are required to report their
compliance to the Accountant General (AG) within four weeks of receipt of
IRs. Serious irregularities are also brought to the notice of the Heads of the
Departments by the AG, Madhya Pradesh through a half yearly report of
pending IRs sent to the Principal Secretary (Finance) .

During 2012-13, nine meetings of the High Power Committee were held in
which 348 IRs (869 paragraphs) were settled.

As of 30 June 2013, 5.404 IRs (21.463 paragraphs) were outstanding against
economic sector departments!. Of these, 3,296 IRs (10,324 paragraphs) were
pending settlement for more than five years. Year wise details of [Rs and
paragraphs outstanding are detailed in Appendix 1.1.

The departmental officers failed 10 take action on observations contained in
IRs within the prescribed time frame resulting in erosion of accountability.

It is recommended that the Government may look into the matter to ensure
prompt and proper response to audit observations.

In the last few years. Audit has reported on several signilicant deficiencies in
implementation of various programmes/activities as well as on the quality of
internal controls in selected Departments, which have negative impact on the
success of programmes and functioning of the Departments. The focus was on
auditing the specific programmes/schemes and to offer suitable
recommendations to the executive for taking corrective and improving service
delivery to the citizens.

Executive Summary

= ‘Works Department Government of Madhya Pradesh, is responsible for
planning, designing and construction of Bridges and Railway over Bridges in
the State. During the period 2008-13, an expenditure of हैं 695.50 crore was
incurred on construction of 385 bridges. The bridge works were funded from
the State Plan Budget, National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development
and Central Road Fund. A performance audit of “Construction of Bridges”
showed deficiencies in planning, execution, contract management and
monitoring of the works.

We observed lack of planning and investigation for construction of bridges as
reflected from selection of unsuitable sites, inappropriate drawings and
designs necessitating post-tender changes and consequent time and cost
overrun as well as inadequate survey leading to delays in execution of bridges.
Government should ensure that detailed survey and investigation of bridge
sites is conducted before preparation of estimates and the designs of bridges
are prepared appropriately.

(Paragraphs 2.1.7.3 to 2.1.7.5)

Execution of bridge works and management of contracts was deficient as
reflected from award of works without land acquisition, delay/non-
construction of approaches of bridges, delay in approval of drawings/designs,
inaction/delay in action on defaulting contractors, which resulted in significant
delays in completion of bridge works. Further, inflated estimation in the cases
of lump sum contracts due to selection of expensive foundation and provision
of unwarranted liners resulted in undue benefit to the contractors and extra
cost to the Department. Government should ensure that construction of
approaches is synchronized with construction of bridges.

(Paragraphs 2.1.8.1 to 2.1.8.6)

Contract management in the construction of bridges was not effective. There
were instances of irregular grant of time extension for delays absolved the
contractors from the liability of penalty besides payment of escalation. The
Department should initiate penal action against the defaulting contractors.

(Paragraphs 2.1.9.1 and 2.1.9.2)



Audit Report Economic (Nan-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Public Works Department (PWD), Madhya Pradesh is the primary agency of
the Government of Madhya Pradesh (GOMP) engaged in planning, designing
and construction of bridges and railway over bridges (ROBs).

Lack of bridges! hinders the development of transportation facilities in a State.
Bridge divisions of the PWD construct bridges sanctioned by the Government.
Other structures are called culverts which are constructed by divisions other
than Bridge Divisions of the PWD.

In order to enhance the transport facilities in Madhya Pradesh (MP), the
Department spent = 695.50 crore on construction of 385 bridges (231
completed including five railway over bridges and 154 ongoing) between
2008-09 and 2012-13. Out of the completed bridges, 92 were completed with
loan assistance from National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development
(NABARD), one from Central Road Fund (CRF) and remaining 138 with
State Plan funds.

PWD is headed by Principal Secretary at Government level. The overall
responsibility of construction of bridges is with the Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C)
who is assisted by a Chief Engineer (CE) Bridge Zone. All bridges are
constructed by the Bridge Zone under the control and supervision of CE,
Bridge Zone Bhopal. CE. is assisted by Superintending Engineers (SEs) in
three circles stationed at Bhopal, Jabalpur and Gwalior. The tield work is
being executed by Executive Engineers (EEs) in eight divisions stationed at
Bhopal, Indore, Ujjain, Sagar, Gwalior, Rewa, Jabalpur and Seoni.

‘The performance audit (PA) was conducted to assess whether:

> adequate funds were available and were properly utilised;

> planning was appropriate and was in accordance with the Indian Road
Congress (IRC) specifications;

> contracts for execution of bridge works were managed as per plan,
standard/approved designs and specifications;

> closure of contracts managed ettectively and according to the terms and
conditions of contracts; and

> monitoring and control system was adequate and effective.

The audit findings were based on criteria derived from the following:

> Provisions of the Madhya Pradesh Works Department (MPWD) Manual;
> Specifications issued by IRC;

1 ‘The term bridge is used where catchment area of water is 15 square km and above and

other structures are called culverts constructed by divisions other than Bridge division
ण PWD.



2008-09

Chapter-I Performance Audit

> Technical instructions and specifications issued by controlling office;
> Terms and conditions of contracts and

> Orders relating to construction of bridges issued by the Government.

Construction of bridges in MP is executed by eight bridge divisions of PWD
under the control of CE Bridge Zone Bhopal. PA was conducted (October
2012 to March 2013) by test check of records relating to construction of
bridges in the offices of CE Bridge Zone Bhopal and all the eight bridge
divisions. Out of 231 completed (2008-09 to 2012-13) and 154 ongoing
bridges (as of March 2013), we examined 81 completed (State 42, NABARD
38 and CRF one) and 22 ongoing bridge works (State 14 and NABARD eight)
selected on random basis.

The audit objectives, criteria and methodologies were discussed with the
Principal Secretary, PWD during the entry conterence held on 25 September
2012. In the exit conference held on 29 August 2013 with the Principal
Secretary, PWD, audit findings were discussed and the views expressed during
the conference have been suitably incorporated in the Audit Report.

The sources of tund for construction of bridges are State budgetary allocation,
loans from NABARD and grant-in-aid from CRF. NABARD financed”
projects have State share of 20 per cent of total project cost. Expenditure
under NABARD assisted projects is incurred first from budgetary allocation of
the State. NABARD reimburses in the form of loan on submission of a
statement of expenditure incurred by the State Government in execution of the
projects.

Funds trom CRF as grant-in-aid for projects are sanctioned by the
Government of India (GOI), Ministry of Road Transport and Highways
(MORT&H), which are routed through Stale Budget. Funds are released by
GOT in instalments on submission of utilisation certificates of earlier released
funds.

2.1.7.1 Selection of bridges for construction

For ensuring development in the State, a master plan should be drawn by the
Department by identifying the requirement of bridges through surveys and its
priority keeping in view the demand of the local people and the limited
resources available. We observed that MPWD Manual is silent about any
norm/criterion for preparation of such master plan. Also. the Department did

construction.
not have any mechanism for assessment and identification of the necessity of
bridges in a State.
We observed that no master plan was prepared of future years for construction
of bridges. For selection of bridge works, EEs forwarded the recommendations
of representatives of local people/Administrative Officers of the districts to CE.
and E-in-C for making budget proposal for a particular bridge. During scrutiny
of this issue in two tests checked districts viz. Balaghat and Indore, we
observed that selection of bridges for construction was not in accordance with
the recommendation made by CE during 2008-09 to 2012-13, as detailed in
the Table 2.2.

2.1.7.2 Preparation of Detailed Project Reports

IRC specification (IRC-SP: 54) provides for preparation of pre-feasibility
report and feasibility report before preparation of Detailed Project Report
(DPR). In the feasibility study, the activities like topographic survey,
hydraulic survey, traffic survey, sub-soil investigations, analysis of data and
identification of alternative bridge site/alignment are required to be performed
for project preparation of the bridge. After finalisation of alignment and bridge
site, detailed investigations are carried out with respect to topographic survey,
sub soil investigation and environmental impact assessment. All these
activities are time taking and it cannot be carried out in short time. CE also
opined (October 2012) in a correspondence with E-in-C that preparation of a
DPR takes two to three months.

E-in-C, PWD issued instructions (22 December 2012) to CE Bridge Zone
Bhopal and the field offices to prepare and submit DPRs of 64 bridge works
by 7 January 2013. After the field offices prepared DPRs, CE technically
sanctioned DPRs within 15 January 2013 and sent the same to E-in-C for
Administrative Approval (AA). Thus, DPRs for 64 bridges were prepared and
scrutinised within a short period of 20 days. Further, AAs of 42 other bridges
were accorded (April 2013) by the Government, in respect of which the details
of preparation of DPRs were not found in records.

Further, according to instructions issued (December 2012) by CE Bridge Zone
Bhopal, site selection for construction of bridge was to be done by the
concerned SE. The site selection for 64 bridges scattered over the State was to
be done by only three SEs. Evidently, it was not feasible to complete site
selection work, detailed survey work and preparation of DPRs within a short
span of time. We observed that in 16 out of 106 bridges approved works had
been stopped due to broad deviations in actual data of site compared to that in
DPRs.

CE stated (June 2013) that DPRs were prepared as per instructions of the
Government and other formalities was to be done at the time of construction
of bridges. In the exit conference (August 2013), the Principal Secretary stated
that on the instructions of the Finance Department, DPRs were prepared in
short period but proper cxamination was done. The reply confirms that DPRs
were prepared in short time in which proper examination was not feasible
resulting in inadequate/ improper preparation of DPRs.

2.1.7.3 Selection of unsuitable sites for bridges led to delay and extra cost

According to IRC specifications (IRC-SP: 54), preliminary surveys of two to
four sites are to be carried out for construction of bridge. Detailed survey of
most feasible and economical site is carried out before preparing General
Arrangement Drawing (GAD) for preparing detailed estimates.

We noticed (October 2012 to January 2013) that in cases of three bridges
(Table 2.3), according to survey report, only one site was considered for
preliminary survey and all the investigations were carried out only for those
single sites. Survey for no other site was conducted to ascertain most feasible
and economical site.

For Malgaon bridge on Pati Bhatki road and Sone bridge on Porhi Rajghat
road, the Department surveyed only one site. On both the selected sites,

2.1.7.4 Inadequate survey & investigation resulting in time and cost overrun
According to IRC specification (IRC-SP: 54), detailed survey and
investigation of the final site is conducted to prepare GAD and estimate for
tendering and execution. Proper survey facilitates preparation of correct
estimates and reduces possibility of post tender changes in the estimates.

Contrary to above, we noticed that in case of 17 bridges awarded during
November 2002 and October 2008, detailed survey for preparation of
departmental GADs was not carried out by the Department. GADs and
estimates were prepared on the basis of preliminary survey reports® only. The
works were awarded to contractors on the basis of GADs so prepared. We
observed that boring for ascertaining sub soil strata on each location, where
foundation was required to be done during survey by the Department was
actually undertaken by the contractors after award of the work. We observed
that CE made changes in the original GAD, which was necessitated after
detailed investigation of strata extracted during execution. Instances of major
deviations such as increase in length of bridges, changes in width, length and
formation level of approaches, inclusion of cement concrete (CC) road,
protection work, toe wall and cross drainage (CD) during execution and

overrun (one month to eight years) and cost overrun of हैं 9.29 crore. Three

0) We observed that in construction of submersible’ bridge across river
Newaj on Bamlabe-Chatukheda road (SI. ४०.14 of Appendix 2.1) provision
of CC road for approaches and its protection work was not made in the
original estimates (November 2002). After construction of the bridge in March
2006, protection work on approaches was executed only in the slope of the
approaches below the embankment height. In the rainy season of 2006, water
topped the bridge as well as approach road and cut the berm? of the road
including pitching work on the slope of the road and finally damaged the
already constructed road work®.

After inspection of the damaged work, SE Bridge Circle, Bhopal and CE
recommended (June and December 2007) execution of protection work up to
embankment height, construction of CC road and a new culvert in the
approach. The protection work, CC road and culvert was executed after
incurring expenditure of हैं 1.02 crore (March 2013). Since the bridge was a
submersible one, protection work was required to be executed up to
embankment height, as recommended later by SE and CE. Non-provision of
CC road for approaches and protection work in the original estimates by EE
indicated lapse in survey work.

We observed that CE issued instructions to construct CC road instead of
Bituminous Top road in approaches of submersible bridges.

On this being pointed out, CE stated (June 2013) that it was a natural calamity
and beyond the control of the Department. In the exit conference (August
2013), the Principal Secretary admitted that there were deviation from DPRs
during execution.

(ii) In another case, we observed in construction of submersible bridge
across Narmada River on Ramnagar-Patpara road (SI. No. 9 of Appendix 2.1)
that in the survey report, essential details viz. rain fall data, flood levels of
previous years were recorded as ‘not known’. CE also pointed out the
deficiencies in the survey report in his letter to EE and instructed (September
2001) him to submit revised GAD after boring of the piers located in the main
part of the river. Boring work was, however, not carried out in the main stream
of the river. During execution, the foundation level of three piers increased by
five metre. On the ground of more depth, change of construction of open
foundation into pile foundation was permitted by CE two years after the
request (December 2006) made by the contractor. Thus, inadequate survey and
investigation, delay in acceptance of drawings and designs resulted in delay in
execution for more than tive years and extra cost of € 86.04 lakh on account of
escalation (€ 40.76 lakh) and change in foundation (© 45.28 lakh).

A submersible bridge is a bridge designed to be overtopped by water during floods.

2.1.7.5 Inappropriate drawings and designs leading to avoidable extra cost
Para 104.6 of IRC 5 stipulates that for calculating the effective linear
waterway”. the width of obstruction due to each pier shall be taken as the
mean submerged width of the pier and its foundation up to the normal scour
level’. Para 104.8.1 of IRC 5 describes that presence of dams, barrages, weirs,
sluice gates ete. on the river affect their hydraulic characteristics like causing
obliquity and concentration of flow, scour, silting in bed, change in flow
levels, bed levels etc. and that these affects shall be considered in the design of
bridges depending upon whether the proposed site of the bridge is upstream or
downstream of a dam.

We noticed that due to reduction in waterway, the bridge works were damaged

causing delay in completion and loss to the Government, as discussed below:

river (Rajdoh Ghat) on Sarni- Ss ae

completed in March 2012 except

(iii) Inordinate delay in approval of drawings/designs

The timely decision taken on the approval of drawings/designs submitted by
the contractors is important to avoid time and cost over-run of the projects.
We noticed in respect of 13 bridge works of six divisions that there were
delays of 60 days to 1,035 days (as calculated by CE Bridge) in approval of
drawings/designs by CE causing time overrun in projects, mainly due to
shortage of technical staff for the work of drawings and designs. The delays
also led to avoidable payment of price escalation of & 80.96 lakh to the
contractors in five 5, 35 detailed in the Appendix 2.4. No ८:

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

(iv) Inaction/delayed action against defaulting contractors led to slippage in
scheduled completion

As per the conditions of contracts, contractor is required to complete works in
time specified therein. According to the clause 1.14 of the agreement, in the
case of slow progress of work by contractors, EE shall have the power to take
possession of the work and take such part of work as remains unexecuted, out
of his hand for giving it to another agency to complete it

We noticed in respect of eight incomplete and two completed bridge works of
seven divisions that contractors did not complete the works within the time
schedule. Due to slow progress of works show-cause notices were issued
repeatedly by EE to the contractors to accelerate the work. However, the
works remained incomplete at the end of scheduled period. Agreements for
four works were rescinded alter lapse of 34 months to 78 months from the
scheduled completion date. In respect of remaining six works, no action was
taken by the Department except issuing show-cause noti Evidently,
inaction/delay in taking action against the defaulting contractors resulted in
delay in completion of works, as detailed in Appendix 2.5.

Two illustrative examples are discussed below:

> The construction of Hathed bridge on Ilarsi-Dolaria road was awarded in
June 2005 for completion by October 2006. Due to slow progress of work,
show-cause notices were issued (December 2005 and June 2006) by EE to
the contractor. CE permitted (August 2006) to rescind the work due to slow
progress on the recommendation of SE, but EE did not rescind the work.
However, EE issued show-cause notices (March 2007 and January 2008) to
the contractor. Later on the request of contractor CE granted permission
(September 2008) to continue the work. The contractor failed to accelerate
the work and EE again issued show-cause notices to the contractor
(October 2008 and July 2010). CE granted permission (August 2010) to
rescind the work but the same was withdrawn at the request of contractor
(September 2010). The contractor could not complete the work and the
work was finally rescinded in January 2012. The balance work was
awarded (April 2012) to another contractor at the risk and cost of the
original contractor for stipulated period of four months but the work was
incomplete as of June 2013. The delayed action by the Department to
rescind the work resulted in delay of more than six years.

> The construction of Pench bridge on Top-Banskheda road was awarded in
January 2008 to a contractor for completion by July 2009. The contractor
could not complete the work in stipulated period. Show-cause notices were
issued (November 2009, January 2011, December 2011 and May 2012) to
the contractor for slow progress of work and the work was rescinded on 13
July 2012 by EE. The rescission was revoked on 25 July 2012 by SE
without assigning any justification and instructed the contractor that work
must be completed by March 2013 and authorised the FF. to take action if
the work remained incomplete. The work was completed in June 2013.
Thus, the work was delayed by 48 months due to delay in taking action
against the defaulting contractor.

2.1.8.2 Inflated estimation due to consideration of well foundation instead of
pile foundation

Technical Sanction for construction of Tamas Bridge on Rajapur-Chilla Road
was accorded by including provision of well foundation and accordingly,
tenders were invited on lump sum basis for the work. However, the contractor
quoted the rate on his own drawing with pile foundation which was accepted
by the Department. The work was completed at a cost of € 15.45 crore in May
2012.

We observed that provision of well foundation (departmental drawing) was
made without ensuring adequate sub-soil investigations. The estimated cost of
the well foundation was हैं 5.12 crore whereas the contractor executed the pile
foundation at a cost of only हैं 3.95 crore. Thus, estimate prepared was highcr
by @ 1.17 crore due to considering well foundation instead of pile foundation.
Had proper estimation and survey been done before acceptance of bid, the
Government could have saved हैं 1.17 crore paid to the contractor on the lump
sum contract.

CE stated (June 2013) that the initial estimate prepared by the Department was
based on insufficient sub-soil investigation. Later when agency was fixed,
boring was carried out at each location and based on these results, the decision
of foundation was taken by the competent authority. He further added (August
2013) that the Government had now stopped calling tenders on lump sum
bas

The fact that the Department has since dispensed with the system of inviting
tenders on lump sum basis indicates that the estimates were inflated and
preparation of inflated estimates without adequate sub soil investigation
resulted in undue benefits to contractor.

2.1.8.3 Non-execution of work as per specifications
(a) Mismatch in the width of approach and carriage way

According to the para 120.1 of IRC-5, the approach roads on either side of a
straight bridge shall have a minimum straight length of 15 metre which shall
be suitably increased where necessary and width of approaches shall be equal
to the width of the bridge.

We noticed in the works of nine bridges that the width of approaches was not
kept equal to the width of the bridges. According to the agreement executed
with the contractors, in three bridges width of approaches was 3.75 m and in
six bridges width of approaches was 7.00 m against width of bridge of 7.5 m
in these nine bridges as detailed in Appendix 2.6. Thus, the construction of
approaches was not in conformity with the specifications and could lead to
accidents due to sudden reduction of road width.

In the exit conference, the Principal Sceretary stated that such deviations from
specifications would be avoided.

(b) Construction of bituminous road instead of Cement Concrete approach
road

For submersible bridges, bituminous road is not suitable because bituminous
topping would get damaged as and when flood overtops submersible
approaches. On the observations of Chief Technical Examiner (CTE), CE
reiterated (in July 2010 and in August 2011) of his earlier instructions (June
2008) to construct cement conerete (CC) road instead of bituminous road in
approaches of all submersible bridges.

We observed in three submersible bridges'* out of 39 submersible bridges that
bituminous road, which was not suitable for submersible bridge, was designed
and constructed on both sides of approach of the bridges instead of cement
concrete 1080.

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that instructions for
construction of CC road in approaches of submersible bridges had again been
issued.

2.1.84 Unfruitful expenditure on constructed bridge due to absence of
approach

The Department issued instructions from time to time that approaches should
be constructed along with bridges. Rectification work is also required to be
completed quickly so that the heneficiaries get benefits of bridges without
delay.

We observed that the construction of high level bridge (without approach) on.
Chhoti Tawa river near Naya Harsood (Chhaneda) was awarded (October
2006) on lump sum basis at a cost of € 6.35 crore to a contractor. The bridge
was completed (June 2011) at an expenditure of € 7.01 crore.

We noticed that, the proposal for construction of approach had not been
initiated as of March 2013. Even after the completion, bridge remained
unused for 21 months (March 2013). Thus, the expenditure of हैं 7.01 crore
remained unftuitful since bridge could not be used by the targeted
beneficiaries.

On this being pointed out, CE stated (June 2013) that process for construction
of approaches of the bridge was being initiated. In the exit conference (August
2013) the Principal Secretary stated that the approach had since been
completed by Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation (MPRDC) and
the details of completion of approach of the bridge would be provided. CE,
MPRDC, however, intimated (November 2013) Audit that the construction of
approach road was in progress.

2.1.8.6 Unwarranted provision of mild steel liner in estimates

Clause 1107 of MORT&H specification for bored cast-in-situ pile foundation
provides that steel lining, required due to soil conditions, be withdrawn after
concreting unless required to be lett in position permanently to avoid the
aggressive action of ground water or in-marine condition.

We noticed that in two bridge works, provisions for steel lining was made in
the estimates without ascertaining its necessity as discussed below:

> In construction of Chhola Railway Over Bridge (Bhopal town) initially,
Open foundation was decided (November 2005) based on trial pit. The
contractor started (August 2006) the work and bored for open foundation
in January 2007. Samples of bore material were sent by contractor to
Indian Institute of Technology (IIT) Mumbai for expert reporting. The

that pile foundation would be the only solution. Therefore, the contractor
requested the Department to permit pile foundation instead of open
foundation. ‘The Department finally agreed (August 2007) to change the
foundation from open to pile.

We noticed that IIT, Mumbai also reported that if six mm steel lining was to
be removed, ground water quality test need to be carried out for chloride and
sulphate ion concentrations. If tests show significant concentrations, sacrificial
(to be abandoned) steel lining need to be provided. We observed that the
Department did not get the tests conducted as suggested by IIT, Mumbai
before making provisions for sacrificial steel lining in the revised estimates.
As sub structure and super structure of the bridge had already been
constructed, removal of steel lining was not possible. Thus, the provision for
sacrificial steel lining at the cost of % 3.32 crore, was not based on
recommended result/test. Against this, an amount of हैं 2.79 crore was already
paid to contractor till March 2013.

On this being pointed out, EE PWD Maintenance Division No.2 Bhopal stated
(January 2013) that the site was quite adjacent to chemical factory and ground
water was contaminated and that as ITT Mumbai had also suggested providing
of liner in piles, the work was carried out accordingly. The reply is not
acceptable as no test of ground water was carried out for ascertaining chloride
and sulphate concentrations in yround water before making provision of
sacrificial steel lining as suggested by IT Mumbai. In the exit conference, the
Principal Secretary however stated that the issue would be examined further
and reply would be sent.

> Construction of ROB of Ratlam-Khandwa section near Banganga was

We observed in the scrutiny of estimate that for pile foundation, provision
of sacrificial steel liner amounting to = 1.53 crore was made in the
estimate. The necessity of sacrificial steel liner or omission of steel liner
in pile foundation could be ascertained only after sub soil investigation.
No report of conduct of soil stability test’® for ascertaining the nature of
soil and necessity of provision of steel liner in the estimates was found
available in survey reports. During execution, CE, after conducting soil
test, observed (July 2008) that the prepared bore hole for execution of
Reinforced Cement Concrete (RCC) cast-in situ pile remains stable and
did not collapse and permitted casting of pile foundation work without the
sacrificial steel liner. The contractor executed the pile foundation of
bridge work without sacrificial steel liner. Thus, inflated estimate was

The Soil Stability Test provides information about the integrity of soil aggregates, and
erosion resistance. It also reflects soil biotic integrity. because the organic matter that
binds soil particles together must constantly be renewed by plant roots and soil
organisms.

2.1.8.7 Less claim of rebate resulted in undue benefit to the contractor

In lump sum contracts, tenders are invited on departmental GAD. Contractors
have liberty to execute the work in lump sum cost by submitting their own
drawings and designs without change in the basic data given in the tender, viz.
type of foundation, length and height of bridge. Drawings and designs
submitted by contractor are scrutinised and approved by CF. Bridge Zone.

Construction of three bridge works, was awarded between March 2007 and
August 2010 on departmental GAD based on well foundation. CE, on the
request of contractors. approved (April 2008 and August 2010) change from
well foundation to pile foundation with following conditions:

> If pile foundation is costlier than the well foundation, extra cost, if any, has
to be borne by the contractor.

> If pile foundation is found cheaper than well foundation, a rebate of
differential amount shall be claimed by the Department as per provisions
of the agreement.

Thus, for the purpose of determining difference in the cost of two types of
foundations, cost of pile foundation actually executed should be compared
with the estimated cost of well foundation based on departmental GAD.

We, however, observed that actual cost of pile foundation was compared with
the cost of well foundation based on contractor’s drawings and designs instead
of that based on departmental GAD. As a result, rebate on these three works
were recovered less from the contractors at the time of making final payments
to them as detailed in the Table 2.7:

‘Table 2.7: Loss due to less claim of rebate

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that calculation of claim

done to find cases of less claim of rebate. He further stated that the system of
awarding lump sum contract had since been discontinued. The reply regarding
claim for rebate is not convincing as claim should have been based on
drawings and designs as per GAD being part of the tender document which

formed basis for quotation by the contractor.

Closing of contract is a process of completing and settling the contract
including rescission of contract as per the contract clause. Instances of
irregular rescission of contracts as well as the irregularities settling the
contract clauses closed contracts resulting in excess and irregular payments to
contractors were noticed, as discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.1.9.1 Irregular rescission of contract resulted in extra expenditure

The construction of high level bridge across Sukni River on Harda-Chhipaner
road (estimated cost हैं 71.55 lakh) was awarded to a contractor ‘A’ at 19 per
cent above SOR (April 2005). Work order was issued (January 2007) to
complete the work within 12 months including rainy season. The contractor
*A’, requested (April 2007) to change the open foundation of the bridge along
with the opinion of Maulana Azad National Institute of Technology (MANIT)
Bhopal that there was a risk of pit collapse in open foundation. But CE Bridge
Zone, Bhopal did not agree (July 2007) with the contractor’s request and
directed the contractor to continue with the drawing supplied. As the
contractor *A’ did not execute any work, the work was rescinded (December
2007) under risk and cost clause. The balance work was re-tendered and
awarded (July 2008) to another contractor ‘B’ at 131.96 per cent above the
estimated cost (@ 71.55 lakh). After the agreement, the contractor ‘B’ also
requested (April 2009) to change the foundation from open to pile. CE Bridge
Zone (successor CE) then agreed and permitted to change the foundation from
open to pile on 20 May 2009. Work was completed in December 2010 and the
final bill for € 2.64 crore was paid to the contractor in March 2012.

Had the decision of change in foundation been taken earlier when requested by

the original contractor ‘A’, the Department could have saved & 1.28 crore'®.

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary accepted the audit observation,

2.1.9.2 Excess payment in violation of contractual clause

According to the clause 4.7.2 of the lump sum agreements, any modifications
required in the design of the piles to conform to the prevailing sound
engineering practice shall be carried out hy the contractors within the lump
sum cost quoted by them for the work. Cast-in-situ concrete piles shall be in
general cast in metal shells which remain permanently in place.

During scrutiny of two bridge works'” executing by Bridge Divisions Rewa
and Sagar, we noticed that contractors modified the type of foundation of the
abutments from open to pile. This was approved by CE. The contractors casted
the piles with the help of liner, which remained permanently in place after
casting of piles. The Divisions paid € 26.37 lakh extra to contractors on
account of steel liner, though the cost was to be borne by them.

EE Bridge Division, Rewa stated (January 2013) that due to collapsible strata,
steel liner was provided as per approval of CF and payment was made
accordingly. EE Bridge Division, Sagar stated (December 2012) that the
matter would be reviewed and action would be taken as per terms and
condition of agreement and extra payment, if any, would be recovered. The
replies are not acceptable as contractors were liable to execute work with steel
liner within the lump sum price quoted by them in terms of the contracts. In
the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that detailed reply would be
furnished. Further reply was awaited (February 2014).

2.1.9.3 Irregular grant of time extension

Clause 1.13 of the lump sum agreement and Clause-2 of the percentage rate
agreement stipulate that if the contractor fails to complete the work within
stipulated period of contract, he will be liable to pay compensation/penalty as
per the provisions of the agreement. Further, he will also be not entitled for
lation for the extended period. Clause 2.3.3 of the agreement
that tenderer should carefully examine the drawings and
specifications and visit the site of works to his satisfaction about nature,
location of the work, the surface condition, ete. which may affect the work or
its cost. Clause 1.13 of the agreement stipulates that the contractor shall not
be entitled to any extension of time in respect of the extra work.

In respect of 15 works in six test checked divisions, we observed that time
extensions without imposition of penalty were granted by CE/SE'* citing
reasons such as, rainy season, rock in foundation, encroachments, standing
crop in field, dacoit affected area, standing water at site, hard strata, extra
work/depth and increase in the depth of foundation which were attributable to
contractors, as detailed in Appendix 2.7. Grant of time extension on these
grounds without penalty, was not justified since completion schedule of the
works was inclusive of rainy season and contractor was supposed to submit
tender only after visit of site to his satisfaction.

2.1.9.4 Excess payment on account of escalation

Clause 2.33 of the agreement provides for adjustment in tender price by way
of payment(s)/refunds arising out of variations in the cost of material, Petrol
Oil and Lubricant (POL), labour, steel and cement for the period from the date
of issue of work order and the date of completion/date of valid time extension.

(i) Excess expenditure due to change in escalation clause of the agreement
Clause 2.33 of the general agreement provides for payment of escalation when
the period of the contract is more than 18 months. As per the clause, escalation
for all the five components, i.e. labour, material, POL, cement and steel was
payable to contractor. Clause-2 of additional special condition of general
agreement provides for payment of price variation. As per this clause, only
difference in prices for bitumen, cement, POL and steel was payable.

In three bridge construction works'”, with completion schedule of more than
18 months, the Department while approving the Notice Inviting Tender (NIT),
scored out escalation component as per clause 2.33 namely, high speed diesel,
cement and steel and allowed adjustment for fuel and lubricants, cement and
steel under modified additional special conditions forming part of agreement.

We observed that in the case of works with completion schedule of more than
18 months, applicability of clause 2.33 or clause 2 of additional special
condition of the agreement had not been clarified in the agreements. This
situation gave a liberty to the approving authority of NIT to score out any of

The Department paid escalation for labour and material as per clause 2.33 of

escalation clause would be ensured in future. The fact thus remains that
adoption of different clauses for payment of escalation for different
component of work without obtaining prior approval of the Finance and Law
Departments resulted in extra expenditure on price escalation.

(ii) Non-consideration of appropriate labour index

Clause 2.33 of the agreement stipulates that while calculating variation in the
consumer price index for Industrial workers, index should be taken at the town
st to the site of the work; the source for such indices being publications
of Labour Bulletin Bureau, Government of India (50) published in the
Reserve Bank of India (RBI) Bulletin.

We observed that in the RBI bulletin, labour indices for only selected cities
were published, which included the index of only Bhopal city in the State of
Madhya Pradesh. Further, the Labour Bureau, Ministry of Labour and
Employment, GOI published indices of all important cities of the States, in
which labour indices of Bhopal, Jabalpur, Indore and Chhindwara were
published. The source of the RBI bulletin is the indices published by Labour
Bureau, Ministry of Labour and Employment, GOL.

Scrutiny of six bridge works”! of two divisions (Rewa and Jabalpur bridge
divisions) indicated that escalation had been paid on labour component by
taking into account the base price indices of Nagpur instead of Jabalpur, which
was nearer to work places at Rewa and Jabalpur. The Department took the
labour index of farther place as per the RBI bulletin, since it did not mention
the indices of the locations where work was undertaken. The selection of

Internal control is a process affected by an organisation's structure, work and
authority flows, people and management information systems designed to help
the organisation in achieving specific goals or objectives, preventing and
detecting fraud and protecting the organisation's resources.

We noticed following weaknesses in internal controls in the Department:

(i) The divisional offices, sub-divisional offices and the works executed by
these offices are required to be inspected by CE, SE and EE as per schedule
laid down in MPWD Manual. CE and EEs conducted the inspections regularly
as per prescribed schedule. As per the Manual, SE was required to inspect
every divisional office at least once in a year, sub division office once in four
years and supervise and inspect the work under his jurisdiction and forward
his report to CE. However, during scrutiny of records in CE’s office no
abstract of inspection reports of particular work/sub-ordinate offices relating
to any inspection by SEs was found, indicating non-conduct of inspection by
SEs.

(ii) | According to the MPWD manual, EE was to ensure that the required
land was available before awarding the bridge works. Instances were noticed
that works were awarded without availability of land {paragraph 2.18.1 (i)}.
Further, pursuance for acquisition of land was done only at EE level and in the
case of delay in acquisition of land, there was no record to show that efforts
were made at SE/CE level by taking up the matter at higher level, which
indicated weak monitoring and control over the functions of land acquisition.

On this being pointed out, CE stated (June 2013) that SEs are being directed to
submit their inspection report to CE office. ‘The reply indicated that inspection
of works as well as subordinate offices by SEs was not ensured by CE. In the
exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that officers would follow the
Manual in this regard.

(iii) Monitoring, in relation to construction works of bridges, includes
supervision of progress of works as per the milestones fixed for timely
completion of any project. We, however, noticed that no targets/milestones for
completion of various stages” of bridge construction works were fixed for
individual bridges to ascertain delay if any and take remedial action.

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that yearly targets had
since been set and records in this regard would be maintained.

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Availability and utilisation of funds for bridge works (2008-13) under
NABARD projects, CRE projects and State projects was reasonably adequate.

Planning and investigation tor bridge works was inadequate as reflected trom
absence of a master plan for construction of bridges and any uniform
procedure for sclection of bridycs, sclection of unsuitable sites for bridges and
inappropriate designs of bridges necessitating subsequent modifications and
consequent time and cost overrun.

Execution of works and management of contract was deficient. Completion of
bridge works was delayed due to delay in land acquisition, approaches not
taken up simultaneously with bridges, inaction against the defaulting
contractors and delay in approving drawings and designs of the bridges. The
benefit of bridges could not be utilised in the absence of approaches.

There were instances of irregularities in closure of contracts in bridge works,
such as irregular rescission of contracts, injudicious grant of time extensions
absolving of the contractors from liability of penalty and entitled them for
receiving escalation and incorrect adoption of price indices in payment of
escalation. Besides, inflated estimation in cases of lump sum contracts resulted
in undue benefits to the contractors and extra cost to the works.

Monitoring of work execution by the Department was deficient as reflected
from non conduct of inspections of works by SEs and lack of pursuance at the
higher level for acquisition of land required for the works.

The Government should ensure the following:

> Preliminary survey of alternate sites and detailed survey and
investigations of selected sites should be conducted before preparation of
estimates. Also, preparation of appropriate drawing and design of bridge
may be ensured to avoid post tender changes and consequent extra cost.

> Construction of approaches should be synchronised with the construction
of bridge works to avoid delay in use of the bridges.

> Penal action should be initiated against defaulting contractors to avoid
delay in completion of bridg

> Strict adherence to the provisions of the agreement should be ensured to
avoid excess payment and undue benefit to contractors.

2013), the reply has not been received so far (February 2014).

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

Executive Summary

= Co-operative Department is responsible for ensuring constitution,
operation, audit and inspection of Co-operative Societies under the
provisions of the Madhya Pradesh Co-operative Societies Act, 1960 and
Madhya Pradesh Co-operative Societies Rules, 1962 as well as
implementation of policies of the Government. The Department implements
various schemes aimed at development through Co-operative Societies,
particularly for the weaker sections and women, As on 31 March 2013, there
were 36,062 working Co-operative Societies registered with the
Co-operative Department, of which nine were apex societies.

Financial management in the Department was deficient. There were savings
under both Plan and Non-Plan allotments. The State was deprived of Central
Assistance for revival of Co-operative Societies and banks since the
Department failed to ensure greater autonomy of Co-operative sector. There
was pendency in recovery of share capital and loans provided to the
Co-operative Societies and banks.

(Paragraphs 2.2.6.1 & 2.2.6.2)
The Department’s performance in discharging regulatory functions was not
satisfactory. It failed to ensure registration of only such societies that
fulfilled the criteria laid down in the Act. There was significant pendency in
audit of the Co-operative Societies. The Government should ensure that the
societies not fulfilling the eligibility criteria are not registered. An annual
plan should be prepared for conducting audit of the Co-operative Societies.

(Paragraphs 2.2.7.1 & 2.2.7.3)
The Department did not take action against the societies for non-
maintenance of records, also against the Board of Directors of Co-operative
Banks for failing in recovery of loans and against the societies for not
holding Annual General Meetings (AGM). This indicated ineffective
management and controls by the Department. Government should initiate
action against the societies for non-maintenance of records, not holding
elections and AGM, as per provisions of the relevant Act.

(Paragraphs 2.2.7.5 to 2.2.7.7)
There were deficiencies in implementation of the programmes for achieving
the Department’s objectives. Integrated Co-operative Development Project
Schemes were not completed within the stipulated period. Share capital and
loans due trom societies were not recovered. The Department admitted the
claim of interest subsidy on the basis of utilisation certificates, without
obtaining audited accounts.

(Paragraphs 2.2.8.1 & 2.2.8.2)
Internal control system in the Department was weak as inspection of records
was not conducted by the Controlling Officers as required in Madhya
Pradesh Treasury Code (MPTC). Government should strengthen monitoring
by regular inspection of records of societies as prescribed under the Act.

(Paragraph 2.2.10.1)



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Co-operative Department (the Department) has the administrative
responsibility of ensuring constitution, operation, audit and inspection of
Co-operative Societies under the provisions of the Madhya Pradesh
Co-operative Societies Act, 1960 (MPCSA) and Madhya Pradesh
Co-operative Societies Rules, 1962 (MPCSR) as well as implementation of
various schemes aimed at developing diiferent classes of the society in the
State, particularly weaker sections and women. The Department assists in
strengthening the Co-operative sector by providing financial assistance to Co-
operative Societies and regulating, controlling and guiding the Co-operative
movement. The main activities of the Department are registration, regulation
under the Act and Rules, audit and inspection, election, disposal of disputes/
appeal, liquidation and cancellation of registration of Co-operative Societies.

Primary Co-operative Societies consisting of farmers/members are registered
to provide assistance to its members. Assistance could be in the form of
financial assistance through loans or in providing fertilizer, seed, assistance in
commodities business, collection, trading of forest produce, consumer goods,
milk collection/distribution, housing, oilseed production, education/training,
etc. Primary Societies are members of Central Societies and Central Societies
are members of Apex Society. As on 31 March 2013, there were 36,062
working Co-operative Societies registered with the Co-operative Department,
of which nine were Apex Soci +

The Co-operative Department in the State is headed by Principal Secretary.
He is assisted by a Commissioner-cum-Registrar of Co-operative Societies
(RCS), who is heading Directorate office. RCS is assisted by 10 Joint
Registrars, 39 Deputy Registrars, 11 Assistant Registrars (Administration) and
50 Assistant Registrars (Audit).

The Joint Registrars function at division level and supervise the work of
District Officers (Deputy Registrars and Assistant Registrars). The Deputy
Registrars and Assistant Registrars (Administration) are responsible for
implementing the schemes for Co-operative Societies, recovery of
Government dues, registering and holding election in the societies, hearing
judicial cases relating to societies. The Assistance Registrars (Audit) are
entrusted with the duty of conduct of audit of societies.

The working of the Department was reviewed to assess whether:

> the financial management was effective and efficient:

culture and Rural Development Bank (iii) M P State Co-operative Federation
(iv) M P State Housing Corporation (v) M P State Co-operative Consumer Federation
(vi) M P State Co-operative Federation Bhopal, (vii) M P State Co-operative Oil
Federation Bhopal. (viii) M P State Seed production and Marketing Federation Bhopal
and (ix) M P State Co-operative Printing Press, Bhopal.

The Government attributed (October 2013) savings to excess provision of Plan
funds and non-occurrence of natural calamities during the years 2010-11 and

under Interest Subsidy Scheme (ISS) to farmers, salaries and wages, non-

Fact remains that budget provision was made to meet natural calamities

funds remaining unutilised and the Department did not surrender such
allotments before the close of the year.

2.2.6.1 Loss of Central assistance due to non-fulfilment of obligations

Under a MoU signed (7 November 2006) among the Central Government, the
State Government and NABARD for revival of the State Co-operative banks,
Central Co-operative Banks and primary agricultural credit societies under
"Vaidyanathan Package", the Central Government and the State Government
were required to contribute हैं 1,652.30 crore and & 162.10 crore respectively.
As outlined in MoU, the Central Government authorised NABARD to
implement the package and released financial assistance, subject to fulfilment
of certain obligations by the State Government and the units in the Co-
operative credit structures.

We noticed that after providing assistance of रैं 985.09 crore during December
2007 to June 2009, Central Government stopped further assistance as the
Department failed to show substantial progress in fulfilling some of the
obligations viz., amendment in MPCSA1960, holding election of Board of
Directors in Primary Agriculture Co-operative Society (PACS) and District
Central Co-operative Banks (Jabalpur and Sagar), appointment of Chief
Executive Officers (CEOs) and professional directors in Co-operative banks,
conversion of 25 per cent of the State Government share into grants,
fulfilment of committed liabilities, determination of further action plan for
closure/merger of ineligible PACS, etc. up to the target date of 31 January
2012. As a result, State Co-operative sector was deprived of Central assistance
of € 667.21 crore for their revival.

The Government stated (October 2013) that the obligations had been fulfilled.

We however noticed that the obligations were fulfilled only by June 2013
against the target date of 31 January 2012 and as a result, the assistance from
Central Government had to be foregone.

2.2.6.2. Non-recovery of share capital, loan and interest from societies

The Department provided financial assistance in the form of loans and share
capital to Co-operative Societies under various schemes”’, which were
recoverable trom the Co-operative Societies in eight equal annual installments
after one year from the date of disbursement of loans.

We noticed that share capital amounting to ₹ैं 38.13 crore out of the total share
capital of = 42.81 crore provided to the societies under 22 units and loans

2.2.6.3 Delayed recovery of interest from the Co-operative Societies

In terms of the MoU (2006), the Government provided (March 2012) loans of
& 38.16 crore to five district co-operative banks under committed liability on
account of the Government guarantee given on behalf of four defaulting
Co-operative Societies” (two working and two under liquidation) for the loans
taken by them. The recovery schedule and the rate of interest on the loans, to
be recovered from the working societies were to be decided after consultation
with the Finance Department.

We noticed that even after lapse of more than one year, no decision was taken
in this regard by the Department as of October 2013.

The Government stated (October 2013) that action for recovery in respect of
two working societies (Madhya Pradesh State Co-operative Consumers
Federation and Jawaharlal Nehru Co-operative Agriculture Produce
Processing Societies) was under process in consultation with Finance
Department and other two societies were under liquidation.

No correspondence was, however, shown to Audit in support of the action
being taken in this regard by the Department.

2.2.6.4 Improper maintenance of cash book

‘As per Rule 53 of MPTC, cash book should be closed at regular intervals and
completely checked. The officer-in-charge of the cash book should verify the
totalling of the cash book or have this done by some responsible subordinate
other than writer of the cash book and initial it as correct. At the end of each
month, the officer-in-charge of the cash book should personally verify the cash
balance in the cash book and record a signed and dated certificate to that
effect.

We observed deficiencies in 16 units out of 37 test checked units, which are
summarised below. Details are given in Appendix 2.10.

issued to DDOs for ensuring compliance with Codal provisions.

(0). Registration of Co-operative Societies

The shortcomings noticed in review in respect of these activities have been
