2.2.7.1 Registration af Co-operative Societies
(9 Registration of ineligible Co-operative Societies

As per the provisions of Section 6 of MPCSA 1960, for registration of a
society, at least 20 persons competent to contract under the Indian Contract
Act and belonging to different families not being in near relations, are
required. As on 01 January 2010, there were 36,391 registered societies, which
rose to 44,267 as on 01 January 2013. Of these. 36,930 were functional and
7,337 were under liquidation.

We noticed in five test checked units that out of total 193 societies registered
during 2012-13 in these units, seven societies were having two or more
members in close relations as declared by the Members (Appendix 2.11).
The Government stated (October 2013) that the registration of seven societies
would be cancelled and corrective measure would be taken in this regard.

(ii) Declining trend in existence of women’s Co-operative Societies

The objective of the Department was to encourage co-operative activities
among the different classes of the society in the State, particularly the weaker
sections and the women.

We observed that there were 5,510 women societies registered at the
beginning of 2010-11. The number of women societies decreased to 5,205
during 2011-12 and further decreased to 5.184 during 2012-13. However, the
Department did not have any plan to encourage constitution of women’s
societies or to revive non-working women’s societies.

On this being pointed out, the Government stated (October 2013) that the
Co-operative Societies are democratic institutions formed voluntarily by its
members, it is mandatory that at least one-third members are female in a
society and as such, there were adequate representations of female members.

The fact remains that the Department did not have any plan to achieve its
stated objective of encouraging constitution of women’s societies or their
revival even though the number of women societies were consistently
declining.

2.2.7.2 Election in the Co-operative Societies

According to Sub section 8(i) of Section 49 of the Act, it shall be obligatory
on the part of the outgoing committee of the society to hold election prior to
the expiration of the term of five years or the extended term. If the society

fails to hold election, the Registrar shall assume charge and hold election as

We noticed that the Registrar assumed charge of 1,900 Co-operative Sacieties
as of August 2013 under the different Sections of the Act but election in
respect of only 75 societies was conducted during the period October 2013.

On this being pointed out, the Government stated (October 2013) that due to
amendment in the Rules consequent to amendment in the Act and delay in
appointment of Election Authority, election in certain societies could not be
conducted in time and the State Government appointed (June 2013) Madhya
Pradesh State Co-operative Election Authority for conducting election in the
societies.

The fact remains that there were delays in holding elections in most of the
societies administration of which was taken over by the Registrar.

2.2.7.4 Disposal of pending judicial cases

Disputes arising among the members of societies and disputes relating to
employment of officials in societies are disposed off in Co-operative Courts
under the provisions of MPCSA 1960.

We noticed that as on 31 December of the years 2010, 2011 and 2012 number
of pending cases were 2.80 lakh, 3.35 lakh and 3.16 lakh® respectively
relating to the varying periods from less than one year to more than 10 years.
During the three calendar years, 94,597, 73,271 and 63,127 cases respectively
were disposed off. Thus, percentage of disposal of the cases was only 25, 18
and 27 respectively during the three year period. Out of the pending cases as
on 31 December 2012, 5,795 cases were of serious nature such as, disputes
regarding terms and conditions of employment, disciplinary action taken by a
society, disputes on constitution, management or business and liquidation of
societies. The cases also included those which were pending for more than 10
years. In the monthly meetings, the Additional Registrar, Co-operative
Societies observed unsatisfactory progress in the matter of disposal of disputes
pending in the Courts.

The Government stated (October 2013) that the number of cases disposed off
during the year 2012-13 was more than the number of cases registered in that
year, it took time in delivering notices to members of societies. It was further
slated that instructions had been issued for quick disposal of the cases.

2.2.7.5 Maintenance of records in societies

Section 56 of the Act provides that every society shall maintain such records,
registers and books of accounts and shall furnish to the Registrar such
information and such returns as may be required by him from time to time. If
any officer or an employee of the society fails to maintain the records,
registers, books of accounts and to furnish to the Registrar such information
and such returns within the specified time, the Registrar may declare such
officer to be disqualified for being a member of the committee for period not
exceeding three years. If the officer is an employee of the society, the
Registrar may impose on him a penalty not exceeding हैं 5,000.

2.2.7.6 Action against short recovery of loans by the Co-operative banks
Section 53(11) of the Act provides that the Registrar shall pass an order for
removal of the Committee or the Managing Committee of the Co-operative
Bank and appoint an administrator for such period or periods not exceeding
one year in the aggregale as may be specilied by the Registrar, if he recovery
of loans during three consecutive Co-operative years is less than 60 per cent of
the annual demand of the banks or if the overdue exceeds 40 per cent.

We noticed in eight units out of 37 test checked units that although the
Co-operative Banks failed to recover a significant part (more than 60 per cent)
of the recoverable amounts during the years 2009-10 to 2011-12 (Appendix
2.13). The Department did not take any action as provided in the Act

The Government stated (October 2013) that as per Section 53 (10) of MPCSA
1960 the Board of Directors of the societies could not be removed without
prior consultation with Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and RBI had not given
any permission for removal of the Board of Directors. However, no records
were shown to Audit to establish that the RBI was consulted for the removal
of the Board of Directors.

Section 49 of the Act stipulates that if defaulted in calling general meeting of
society within three months before close of financial year, the Registrar may
declare the responsible officer disqualified for being elected or being in office
for a period not exceeding three years.

We noticed in six units* that although 686 primary agricultural credit
Co-operative Societies failed to conduct the annual general meetings during
the period 2009-12, the Department did not take action as provided in the Act.

2.2.81 Integrated Co-operative Development Project (ICDP) Scheme

ICDP, a Centrally Sponsored Scheme, was in operation in the State since
1985. The Scheme aims at overall development of economic activities on
co-operative basis in selected districts in the area of agriculture, agro-allied
and co-operative banking etc. Under the Scheme, financial assistance to
Co-operative Societies is provided in the form of loan, share capital and
subsidy for development of infrastructure, business and human resources.

the State Government is to implement ICDP through the Project
Implementing Agency* (PIA) which is responsible for overall implementation
of the project. PIA and Project Implementation Team (PIT) appointed by PIA.
are required to submit monthly progress report and annual monitoring report
to National Co-operative Development Co-operation (NCDC).
Implementation of the Scheme is monitored by a State monitoring cell.

Funding pattern by NCDC to the State Government and by the State
Government to Co-operative Societies for the schemes/activities” under ICDP
is given in Appendix 2.14. Release of assistance from NCDC is progress
based and on reimbursement basis. Thus, the State Government has to first
provide funds to PIA and then seek reimbursement from NCDC.

In the 117 Five Year Plan (2007-12), 15 districts were selected in the State.
NCDC sanctioned % 188.52 crore for implementation of five activities under
ICDP. Against the sanctioned amount, हैं. 183 crore was allotted by the State
Government to PIA, which could spend & 175.05 crore (up to 31 March 2013)
for the Scheme, as detailed in Appendix 2.15. We noticed shortcomings in
implementation of the Scheme as discussed below:

“© The State Government appoint/nominate PIA in each District which is generally

District Central Co-operative Bank (DCCB) or branch of the State Co-operative Bank
or State Co-operative Marketing Federation or any other similar organisation,
nominated by the Government in consultation with NCDC.

(i) Civil works, plant and machinery, infrastructure. facilities (ii) Margin money to
societies, (iii) Share capital to DCCRs/PIA, (iv) Man power development and training
(v) Managerial assistance to PIA and Monitoring Cell

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

(i) Delay in completion of ICDP schemes

NCDC sanctioned (March to November 2007) 15 ICDP schemes, one each for
the 15 districts selected for implementation within five years. As per the terms
and conditions of ICDP, the project period of ICDP varied between four years
and five years depending upon the outlay, activities and volume of
infrastructure to be created under ICDP.

During scrutiny of records of RCS and the Department Headquarters, we
noticed that in 15 districts, despite spending € 175.05 crore, none of the
projects could be completed within the stipulated period (completion schedule
between March 2012 and November 2012). While ICPD schemes in three
districts (Vidisha, Jhabua and Mandsaur) were completed with delays ranging
from 6 to 12 months, the projects in 12 districts were not completed as of
November 2013. The Deputy Director (Project) attributed (July 2013) the
delay to the fact that from the year 2008-09, funds received from NCDC were
kept in the departmental plan by the State Planning Commission. Since the
Commission did not enhance the Plan limit, provision was not made in the
State budget according to the target set. Since, expenditure on main activities
of ICDP was to be financed by NCDC on reimbursement basis, initial
induction of funds by State Government would have facilitated timely
completion of the Scheme.

The Government stated (October 2013) that provisions in State Budgets were
not according to the targets for ICDP scheme for the years 2008-09 to
2010-11. Therefore, it was imperative to increase the completion period to
achieve remaining targets of the schemes.

The reply confirms the fact that there was delay in release of funds, which
contributed to delay in completion of the projects under the scheme.

(ii) Non recovery of share capital, loans and interest under ICDP schemes

ICDP scheme provided for interest free share capital and interest bearing loan
to Co-operative Societies, which are to be refunded to the State Government in
eight equal annual instalments alter one year and three years respectively from
the date of disbursement of share capital and loans. Interest on loans is
charged at the effective rate indicated in sanctioned orders applicable at the
time of disbursement of loan. In case of failure to pay the instalments of loan
on or before the due date, interest rate is increased by one per cent and penal
interest of 2.5 per cent is also charged.

The following observations were made in audit:

> In 13 districts, share capital of हैं 47.62 crore was provided to
Co-operative Societies during 1996-97 to 2006-07. As on 31 March 2013,
an amount of & 32.69 crore was to be recovered from the societies, of
which हैं 24.15 crore was recovered, as detailed in Appendix 2.16.

> In eight districts, loans of हैं 6.05 crore was provided to Co-operative
Societies during 1995-96 to 2006-07. The outstanding amount of loans
and interest (including penal interest) was not recovered from the Co-
operative Societies was हैं 3.65 crore, as detailed in Appendix 2.17.

2.2.8.2 Interest subsidy scheme for member farmers of Co-operative
Societies

The State Government introduced (2006-07) Centrally Sponsored Scheme ot
Interest Subsidy in respect of short term production credit (crop loan) to
farmers. Under the Scheme, member farmers of Co-operative Societies are
provided short term crop loan on concessional interest rate (zero per cent to
three per cent) by State Co-operative Banks through PACS. The difference**
of base interest (11 per cent during the three years) and interest payable by
farmers is paid to banks by Central Government and State Government as
interest subsidy’. The claims of interest subsidy were to be submitted by
PACS and the District Central Co-operative Banks through Madhya Pradesh
State Co-operative Banks.

Under the above scheme, the State Government paid interest subsidy of
& 150.46 crore, हैं 282.11 crore and हैं 328.12 crore to 25 lakh, 29 lakh and 30
lakh farmers respectively during the years 2010-11, 2011-12 and 2012-13.

The following observations were made:

> The Department admitted the claim of interest subsidy on the basis of
utilisation certificates given by Apex bank. In terms of Government order
(10 January 2012), interest subsidy was to be reimbursed on the basis of
audited accounts of PACs and only in respect of eligible farmers. The
Department, however, did not obtain audited accounts of claims for
interest subsidy to examine the correctness of the claims and did not
ensure that interest subsidy was not paid in respect of ineligible farmers.

> For the amount of interest subsidy due between 15 March 2011 and 15
June 2013, the State Government paid % 760.69 crore”” between July 2010
and March 2013 to Apex Bank for disbursement to PACs in the State.
Thus, the amount was paid to Apex Bank 7 days to 258 days before the
due date instead of reimbursing the interest subsidy after the due date.
Total interest on loans © 760.69 crore) for these periods (7 to 258 days)
works out to € 10.45 crore at average interest paid on borrowed funds".

The Central Government pays 3.5 per cent to 5 per cent and the State Governments
pays 4.5 per cent to 6.5 per cent as interest subsidy on account of difference of normal
interest and interest payable by farmers,

During the year 2011-12, total interest payable to Co-operative Banks by Central/State
Government was 11.5 per cent against the hase rate of |] per cent as per the
Government letter dated 10 January 2012 and information provided by the Department

2.2.10.1 Internal Audit

Internal audit is an important tool for the Management to ascertain the
accuracy and effectiveness of the internal control mechanism. The
Department, however, did not have any set up for internal audit. Besides, as
provided in the Process Manual of Finance Department, annual audit roaster is
required to be prepared by the Directorate of Treasuries and Accounts for
conduct of audit of Head of Department Offices. We also noticed that audit of
the Head of the office of Co-operative Department was not conducted by the
Audit team of the Finance Department during the period of review.

2.2.10.2 Inspection by Controlling Officer

Rule 293 of MPTC provides that the Controlling Officer should conduct
annual inspection of the records to be maintained by his subordinate DDOs.

We noticed in 35 units that inspection was not conducted by the Controlling
Officers (Joint Registrar and Registrar) during the period of audit.

The Government stated (October 2013) that instructions had been issued for
carrying out inspection.

2.2.10.3 Non-verification of challans received on account of receipts of
audit fees from the societies

According to Rule 30 of MPFC (Vol.)), all the receipts regarding payment of

Government dues during a month should be verified from the records of the

treasury concerned.

We observed (July and August 2013.) in offices of Assistant Registrar (Audit)
at Sagar and Bhopal that the Co-operative Societies sent copies of challans to
district offices as proof of remittance of audit fees (Sagar: हैं 84.86 lakh during
April 2009 to March 2013 and Bhopal: हैं 60.89 lakh during April 2011 to June
2013). However, verification of actual receipt of audit fees in the government
account was not carried 001 {rom the records of treasuries. In the absence of
prescribed verification, possibility of non-payment/short-payment of audit fees
cannot be ruled out

On this being pointed out, the Government stated (October 2013) that
verification in respect of Sagar unit had been done and in respect of Bhopal
unit it was heing done. It was further stated that standing instructions were
issued and was reiterated.

The fact remains that since delay in veritication of remittance in treasury
would result in non-deduction/delayed detection of misappropriation of the
Government money.

The Co-operative Department entrusted with the responsibility of ensuring
constitution, operation, audit and inspection of Co-operative Societies under
the provisions of Madhya Pradesh Co-operative Societies Act, failed to ensure
registration of only such societies that fulfilled the criteria laid down in the
Act. Provisions regarding conduct of election of societies, timely audit of
societies, compliance to issues raised in audit, record maintenance and conduct
of AGMs by societies were also not monitored and enforced by the
Department.

45


Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

There was shortage of manpower in many cadres of the Department adversely
affecting its functioning.

Internal control system in the Department was weak as it was not having any
set up for internal audit and inspection of records was not conducted by
DDOs as required in MPTC.

The Government should consider the following:

> Ensure that societies not fulfilling the eligibility criteria are not allowed to
be registered and put in place a monitoring mechanism to review the
continued fulfillment of eligibility criteria of existing societies;

> Prepare an annual plan for conducting audit of Co-operative Societies, as
and when due;

> Initiate necessary action against societies for non-maintenance of records.
not holding elections/AGMs in terms of the provisions of the Act; and

> Strengthen monitoring by regular inspection of records of societies as
prescribed under the Act.

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

Executive Summary

Management of forest offences is one of the primary responsibilities of the
Forest Department. The management and protection of forests is regulated
mainly by the Indian Forests Act, 1927. A review of management of forest
offences and protection of forest in the State was conducted to assess
adequacy and efficacy in prevention, detection, registration, investigation,
disposal and monitoring of forest offences by the Department.

Number of encroachment cases doubled during 2008-12 and 51 per cent
wireless sets were not in working condition, affecting the prevention of forest
offences. There was significant shortfall of staff in the working strength for
management of forest offences. The Department did not ascertain whether the
vehicles hired for patrolling of sensitive area were used for the intended
purpose. Government should review off the trend of various offences and
frame offence-specific plan for effective protection of forest offences.
(Paragraphs 2.3.7.1 to 2.3.7.4)
Detection and registration of forest offence was deficient. Use of satellite data
tor detection of offences was not adequate. There was significant shortfall in
beat inspections by the departmental officers. Registration of forest offence
cases was not monitored due to non-submission of counterfoils of preliminary
offence reports to the Division. There were significant delays in registration of
cases. Government should ensure optimum use of available infrastructure of
Information Technology for timely detection of forest offences and
strengthening the system of registration of cases.
(Paragraphs 2.3.8 and 2.3.9)
Investigation of forest offences for assessing the loss of forest product was
deficient. There was no uniform procedure to ascertain quantum of actual loss
of timbers. The quantity of minerals extracted through illegal mining was not
reported in large number of cases. This also resulted in short recovery of
losses. Government should ensure formulation of uniform procedure for
valuation of loss af timber and the lass due to illegal mining.
(Paragraphs 2.3.10.4 and 2.3.10.5)
There were deficiencies in disposal of offence cases and seized material.
Department could not formulate reasonable basis for classification of cases on
basis of gravity of offences for compounding or prosecution of offence cases.
We observed that large number of forest offence cases were time barred due to
delayed investigations and submission to Courts. Government should
streamline the system of disposal of forest offence cases within the time limit.
(Paragraph 2.3.10.3)
Monitoring of forest offence cases was deficient as the inspection reports for
controlling illegal mining were not submitted to the Government. Forms for
reporting of offences did not provide important information. Besides, timber
accounts were not prepared for long period. Government should strengthen the
system of reporting by using suitable forms and Forest Offence Management
System (FOMS).
(Paragraph 2.3.12)

47


Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Madhya Pradesh has a forest area of 94,689 sq. km. More than 41 per cent
villages (21,797) in the State are located within 5 km of forest areas and
around one crore people are dependent on forests for their livelihood. As per
the Annual report on forest offences prepared by the Principal Chief
Conservator of Forests (PCCF), 16.37 lakh trees were felled. 13,845 hectares
(ha) of forest land was encroached, 13,970 ha of area was illegally mined
while 1.57 lakh ha area was affected by forest fire during the calendar years
2008-12.

According to Indian Forests Act, 1927 (IFA), any action causing any damage
by negligence or deliberately in felling any tree or dragging any timber,
grazing of cattle, grass cutting, quarrying of stones, making encroachments
etc. in forest area is an offence. Illegal possession and transport of forest
produces, encroachments on forest land for any purpose, and setting forests to
fire are also offences under IFA. Forest Officers are vested with powers to
enquire into offence cases, compel the witness to attend the court, arrest
without warrant, seize forest produce suspected to be illegally obtained.
confiscate vehicles and other property used in the commission of forest
offences, eviction of forest encroachers, and also to dispose off cases under
these laws. Madhya Pradesh Forest Policy (MPFP), 2005 envisages
strengthening of infrastructure for protection of forest and stern action against
person indulging in forest offence and their abettors.

During the period 2008-13, an expenditure of हैं 94.64 crore was incurred for
protection of forest and detection of forest offences, under the schemes
Intensification of Forest Management (IFM) (® 44.87 crore) and Strengthening
of Administration (€ 49.77 crore).

The Department functions under the overall control of the Principal Secretary
at Government level. The Principal Chief Conservator of Forest (PCCF) is
responsible for overall administration of the Department. He is assisted by
Additional Principal Chief Conservator of Forests/Protection (APCCF) who is
entrusted with responsibility of giving direction and control of forest offences.
An organogram reflecting the organisational structure for management of
torest offences up to the beat level is given below:

Prevention of offence is a prime component of forest protection. For
prevention of offences, the Department procured wireless sets, deployed
manpower for patrolling and hired vehicles for patrolling. Inadequacies
observed in prevention of offences are discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.3.7.1 Use of wireless sets in forest protection

According to MPEP, 2005, use of wireless network and other communication
facilities would be extended to strengthen the forest protection system.
Further, PCCF directed (April 2009) the Divisions to prepare communication
plan to maintain connectivity between officers and staff. It was also decided
optimise the use of mobile phones while maintaining minimum use of
wireless, as mobile connectivity may not be available at interior forest areas.
We observed (between October 2012 and June 2013) in 21 test checked
divisions that 1,694 wireless sets (hand set, fix set and mobile sets) were
available, of which 862 sets were not in working condition. Besides, license
fee and royalty due according to Indian Telegraphy Act (ITA) was not paid
regularly to the Government of India (GOI). We further observed that GOL
had issued (October 2013) notices to the Department for payment of
accumulated dues amounting to = 31.98 crore. The licenses were liable for
cancellation if dues were not paid immediately. Department has so far not paid
the dues and cancellation of licenses by GOI would adversely impact the
management of forest offences.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that due to non-payment of license tee and
royalty, renewal of license and repair of old wireless sets was not possible and
efforts were being made to restore wireless system. In view of the fact that

Deployment of adequate man power is vital for protection related activities

2.3.8.2 Shortfall in beat inspection

According to the instructions issued by the APCCF/Protection (June 2004 and
January 2009), forest officers of the level of RA to DFO were to conduct beat
inspections periodically, as prescribed in beat roaster, with the objective of
controlling various forest offences.

We observed (between October 2012 and June 2013) in scrutiny of reports on
beat inspections in six™ out of 21 divisions that during the period between
2008 and 2012, there was shortfall in beat inspection ranging from 11 per cent
to 40 per cent in four divisions by SDO, two to 22 per cent in six divisions by
RO, four to 22 per cent in five divisions by RA while it was 30 per cent and
41 per cent in two divisions by DFO, as shown in Appendix 2.20.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that shortfall in beat inspection was due to
utilisation of field staff by District administration in elections and managing
law and order. The reply is not convincing as the deficiency persisted during
the period 2008-12, despite being pointed out in previous Audit Report (2003).

After discovering commission of an offence, a Preliminary Offence Report

2.3.9.1 Non-submission of Preliminary Offence Report to Divisions

The Department instructed (October 2004) that Forest Guards should send a
counterfoil of Preliminary Offence Reports (PORs) to DFO to enable him to
monitor the cases on a day-to-day basis and conduct enquiry under his
supervision in cases of serious nature of offences.

There was nothing on record to show that the counterfoils of PORs were being
sent to DFOs except in Sheopur Division. In absence of counterfoils of PORs
at division level, it could not be ascertained by DFOs whether all PORs have
been registered in Range Offices while checking the monthly report on
registered forest offence cases of the Ranges.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that adequate transparency was adopted for
registration of cases through computerised system, FOMS. All cases were
registered in computers which were available for monitoring by DFO. Hence,
there was no need to send the counterfoils to DFO. During exit conference, it
was stated that a mechanism would be evolved for ensuring timely delivery of
POR counterfoils. The fact remains that due to non-submission of counterfoil
of PORs to DFO in 20 divisions, registration of each case was not ensured
through cross checking of counterfoils with entries in FOMS.

2.3.9.2 Lack of uniformity in formats used for Forest Offence Case
Register
The Forest Offence Case Register (FOCR) is a basic record maintained by the
Range Offices which provides status of cases at a glance. Format for FOCR
has not been provided in MPFM. The Department, however, prescribed a 35
column format for FOCR in May 2003 containing information such as date of
registration of the case, brief about the case, name of co-operating Joint Forest
Management Committees (IFMCs), type of offence, seized material, date of
handing over the case to Investigating Officer, date of return of case after
investigation, further action taken for disposal through compounding or
submission to court.

We observed (October 2012 to June 2013) in 21 test checked divisions that
different formats consisting of 7 to 57 columns were used in 15 divisions. In
12* divisions, FOCRs contained 7 to 30 columns only. Thus, there was no
uniformity in practice in maintenance of FOCR in the Ranges and possibility
of omission of important information such as, valuation of seized material,
recovery of loss, remittances of loss recovered in banks/treasury etc. in the 12
divisions using formats having less than 35 columns, cannot be ruled out.
Absence of various information prescribed in standard formats would cause
hindrance in effective monitoring of disposal of the registered offence cases.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that instruction was issued to all the Divisions
for use of standard formats of FOCR. The fact remains that prescribed format
was not being used by all the divisions.

2.3.9.3 Delay in registration of the cases

According to Para 77 (3) of MPFM, a forest guard discovering the
commission of an offence must, within 48 hours of such discovery, report it to
RA or to RO.

Scrutiny of FOCRs in 21 audited divisions indicated (between October 2012
and June 2013) that except in Indore division, the cases were reported within
10 days. FOCRs for 2008 and 2009 of Mahu Range in Indore Division
however, revealed that reporting of 40 out of 564 cases registered was delayed
by 16 days to five months resulting in delay in initiating enquiry of the cases.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that instruction was issued for timely reporting
of discovered cases. The reply is not convincing as the deficiency persisted
despite being pointed out in previous Audit Report (2003).

2.3.9.4 Non-registration of offence cases of girdling

According to Section 26 (f) of IFA, girdling® of tree is a forest offence,
punishable up to one year, or fine or both.

Scrutiny of files/ records relating to special permission of removal of felled
trees and FOCR of various Ranges in Dewas division indicated (January 2013)
that girdling of 218 sound’ Teak trees was noticed (between October 2010
and June 2012) during patrolling of Kannod and Wagli Ranges and Khiwni
Sanctuary. We, however, observed that offence cases of girdling of 20 trees in
Khiwni Sanctuary were not registered. DFO also did not take cognizance of
the fact after receipt of request (June 2012) from range office regarding felling
of girdled trees, Non-registering of the offences resulted in non- investigation
of the same.
PCCF stated (September 2013) that the matter was being enquired into with
concerned CF, Ujjain and instructions for registering all cases of girdling of
trees would be issued

IFA empowers the Forest Department and defines procedures for investigation
and compounding of cases of forest offences. During the period” 2009 to
2012, total 2,66,575 forest offence cases were registered. During this period,
2,67,862 (91 per cent) cases were investigated and 2,14,461 cases (73 per
cent) were compounded including 28,188 pending cases as on 31 December

able to arrest increase in the pendency for investigation of forest offence cases,
we noticed that 5,592 cases became time barred as discussed in para 2.3.10.3

Deficiencies noticed in investigation and disposal of forest offence cases are
discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.3.10.1 Revocation of forest offence cases

Sections 26 and 33 of IFA provides for penalty or imprisonment or both. The
Act, however, does not provide for revocation of the cases in any
circumstances.

We noticed that the Department neither classified the minor offences
or major offences nor formulated any reasonable basis for identifying the
cases to be compounded by the Forest Officer or to be prosecuted in the court
of law under IPC or IFA on the basis of the classifications so that perpetrators
of major offences are given exemplary punishment.

We observed that as per the Government order (August 2008), 66,253 forest
offence cases were revoked. Out of these, 21,296 cases of encroachments
registered up to 13 December 2005 were regularised under the Scheduled
Tribes and Other Traditional Forest dwellers (Registration of Forest Rights)
Act, 2006. The basis/justification for revoking these cases and not submitting
the cases to the courts for prosecution could not be ascertained in audit.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that petty cases of forest offences were
revoked to establish coordination between forest officials and people living in
the area nearby the forest. The fact, however, remained that there was no
provision in the Act for revocation of cases under any circumstances without
any penalty and compensation even for petty cases of forest offences.

2.3.10.2 Delay in disposal of forest offence through judicial process

MPEP, 2005, envisages constitution of Special Court for speedy disposal of
forest offences. In addition, summary trial of forest offence cases is also
provided in IFA, 1927.

We did not notice setting up of Special Courts and adoption of summary trial
procedure for disposal of forest offence cases in any of the audited divisions.
The Department did not also constitute any Body/Cell/Wing and posted ACFs
at Division level for facilitating prosecution of forest offences or hired
services of lawyer.

We further noticed, (between October 2012 and June 2013) in 20 (except

process of disposal through Court of law was started in 4,076 cases®. Only
911 cases were settled during the period and 3,165 cases were pending in
courts (December 2012). Setting up of Special Courts or adoption of summary
trial procedure could speed up the disposal of pending cases of forest offences.

During the exit conference, it was stated that setting up of Special Courts was
an old demand of the Department and ACF were posted in divisions. But, due
to excessive regular work, it was difficult to monitor the court cases related to
forest offences. The reply, however, does not explain the measures proposed
by the department for speedy disposal of forest offence cases and reduce
number of pending cases.

2.3.10.3 Time barred forest offence cases

For offences registered under IFA, 1927, maximum punishment is up to one
year. Therefore, the cases registered under the said Acts will become time
barred” within one year. The Government directed (April 2003) that offence
cases should be disposed off within the prescribed period by way of
compounding or should be brought to Court of law for prosecution.

We noticed (July 2013) from the status of disposal of the cases provided by
the APCCF/Protection that 5,592 cases became time barred in the Department
between 2009 and 2012" due to non-completion of investigation,
compounding and non-submission of the cases to courts within the stipulated
period prescribed in CrPC.

PCCF agreed (September 2013) that some cases became time barred due to
engagement of staff in other works and stated that investigation and disposal
of cases within time limit was necessary. The reply confirms that the
deficiency is persisting despite assurances given by the Government to PAC
that the Department would fully endeavour to dispose of cases under IPC
within the time limit.

2.3.10.4 Inappropriate procedure in calculation of loss in cases of illicit
felling

According to GOMP, Forest Department order (February 2004) regarding
fixing of responsibilities for illicit felling, loss is to be calculated after
deducting commercial value of seized forest produce from the vriksha mulya”
of the girth” class of the felled tree, fixed by CFs, while the girth classes were
not prescribed. We observed the following:

> According to the system prevalent in the Department for calculation of
loss, the divisions reduce girth class by one level after taking actual
measurement of the stump of felled tree. We noticed from the schedules

2.3.10.5 Measuring seized material in standard unit.

According to Sec. 2(4) (iv) of IFA, forest produce include peat, surface soil.
rock and minerals (including lime-stone, laterite, mineral oils and all products
of mines or queries). Illegal mining also affect adversely the soil and moisture
conservation, ecology and environment. In cases of forest offence, the forest
produce recovered, if any, is seized and confiscated after investigation and
transported to depots tor disposal. Annual physical verification of various
forest produces lying in depots is also required to be conducted by SDOs.

We observed that the Department did not prescribe standard unit for
measuring seized material and calculation of loss in cases of illegal mining, In
21 divisions, 1,264 cases of illegal mining involving 2,224.277 ha mined
area™ were reported during 2008-2012. The quantity of minerals extracted
and loss thereayainst was, however, not reported in these cases. In the absence
of extracted quantity of illegal mines, quantum of loss of forest produce to
State caused by illegal mining could not be estimated. We observed the
following:

> According to FOCR in Raisen Division, 86 cases of illegal
mining/transportation of forest produce were registered between 2008 and
2011. The Division seized 2,916 stone slabs, 11 tractors trolley movrum,
two tractors trolley boulders and 2,10,000 bricks.

Seized forest produce was transported to various depots but the material
was not measured in standard units (cu m or cu ft). Periodic physical
verification of the produce was also not done at depots between 2008 and
2011. In the absence of physical verification of seized material, actual
quantity available could not be verified. Records relating to transportation
and disposal of seized material were also not produced to Audit.

On this being pointed out, DFO stated (March 2013) that the seized material
was measured in numbers and transported to Raisen depot and physical
verification would be conducted measuring in cubic feet/metre. He also stated
that the cases were disposed off based on material at site and that manipulation
of forest produce had not come to notice. The reply is not acceptable as
without measurement of illegally mined area; it would not be possible to know
the actual loss of forest produce.

2.3.10.6 | Non-reclamation of mines

According to standard conditions prescribed for according clearance of
diversion of forest land for mining purposes under the Forest (Conservation)
Act, 1980, phased reclamation of mined area is to be done to minimise impact
of mining in soil and moisture conservation, ecology and environment.
However, there is no specific provision in IFA, for compensatory afforestation
in case of illegal mining.

We observed that there was no mechanism in the Department for ensuring
reclamation of illegally mined area. As per information furnished by the 21
test checked divisions, 2,224.277 ha area was affected in illegal mining during
2008-2012, but no reclamation in the areas was done, except in compartment
number 392 in Raisen Division. Absence of reclamation would have adverse
impact on soil/moisture conservation, ecology and environment in these areas.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that reclamation in some areas was done
according to availability of funds. The reply confirms that there was no
provision to recover the cost of compensatory afforestation from the offenders
involved in illegal mining and reclamation of land was thus dependent on the
funds available with the department for compensatory afforestation.

23.111 Deficiencies in disposal of cases of illicit felling

Illicit felling is the offence with highest occurrence among forest offences.
We observed in 21 divisions that total 1,58,859 cases of forest offences were
registered during January 2008 to December 2012. Out of this, 1,39,355
cases (88 per cent) of illicit felling of 7,92,092 trees were registered.
Following deficiencies were noticed in these divisions in follow up of
registered offence cases of illicit felling:

(i) Lack of deterrent action in the cases of organised offence

Targets of beat inspection were fixed by PCCF/ Protection (June 2004) for
various level of Forest Officers, who directed that DFO shall inspect any beat
on his discretion but special attention on sensitive beats should be given.

Tn Piprani North and Deopura Beats in Budera Range of Sheopur Division,
illicit felling of total 5,167 trees were noticed between 18 and 21 March 2010.
Period of occurrence of illicit felling was estimated between one month and
two years and oftenders were identified as a group of villagers of Kurrai, who
cut the trees for encroachment in forest land. Deopura Beat was marked as
sensitive one and was to be included in the roster of beat inspection by DFO,
but it was not included in the roster. According to the roster the RA and ROs
inspected the beat during the period of the illicit felling. Despite this, the
offences remained undetected.

These cases were submitted (March 2010) in Court of law under IFA, 1927 in
which maximum provision of one year imprisonment was provided. The
Department also had option to lodge complaint of thelt of forest produce with
police under IPC in which punishment of up to three years is provided. The
Department had, however, not formulated any policy/guidelines for deciding
to invoke provisions of either of the two Acts.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that many forest areas in the State were highly
sensitive for illicit felling, offences increased due to organised group or in
provocation by self help groups, attacks on forest officials had also increased
and illicit felling had increased despite efforts by the Department. He further
stated that action was taken in cases of illicit felling according to prescribed
tules.

The reply, however, is silent about the observation made in audit.
(ii) Short assessment and recovery of compensation in forest offences

According to Section 68 (as amended vide GOMP notification, dated 27
March 2010) of IFA, for compounding a forest offence, compensation leviable
should not be less than double the value of the forest produce. The Act further
empowers that property seized in forest offence may be released on payment
of the value thereol'as estimated by Forest 011०0:

(iv) Shortfall of confiscated material in re-measurement

The Forest Department, GOMP, directed (February 2004) that Forest Officers
shall be held accountable for any loss due to illicit felling according to duties
discharged by them for protection of area under their control. As per directions
issued by the Department (July 2003), loss on account of illegally fell trees _is
worked out by deducting the value of confiscated timber measured at sale
depot from the value of illicitly felled trees, based on rates of timber fixed by
the Department. In January 2005, PCCF observed that there were shortfalls in
volume of confiscated timber at sale depots compared to the volume measured
at place of offence due to measuring the logs without removing the bark. He,
therefore, ordered that action for recovery of loss due to such shortfall should
be reconciled with the records of the measurement recorded at sale depots and
recovery of the loss due to such shortfall should be initiated.

We observed (between October 2012 and June 2013) from the records relating
to confiscated timber in 117 out of 21 test checked Divisions that between
2008 and 2012, total 3,000.898 cu m of seized timber, as measured at the place
of offence was transported to sale depots of the concerned divisions. In the re-
measurement of the seized timber at sale depots, quantity was found 2.467.767
cu m, i.e. a shortfall of 533.131 cum. We observed that loss of the timber was
calculated taking into account the measurement done at place of offence
instead of measurement done at sale depots, in violation of the directions of
the Department, which resulted in short assessment of हैं 1.09 crore
(Appendix 2.24) and consequent short recovery.

(v) Failure in dressing of stumps of illicitly felled trees
APCCF (Protection) issued (January 2011) instructions that stumps of illicitly
felled trees should be dressed”! and that the offence cases be compounded only

atter completion of stump dressing. A certificate to this effect was to be
recorded by RO.

The scrutiny of records of 21 divisions indicated (between October 2012 and
June 2013) that in 109 forest offence s of seven” divisions, 1,265 trees
were reported as illicitly telled between October 2008 and November 2012
and disposed of between March 2011 and February 2013. There was no record
to show that stumps of these trees were dressed before disposal of the cases as
per the instruction of APCCF (Protection). This adversely affected
regeneration process of the illicitly felled trees.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that due to lack of budget, stumps could not be
dressed. The reply, however, silent about the reasons for non-provision of
funds and does not address the issue of adverse impact on regeneration of
illicitly felled trees due to failure in stump dressing.

2.3.11.2 Deficiency in fixation of commercial rates

Forest Department, GOMP directed (September 1985) that commercial rates
for valuation of trees should be tixed before commencement of each forestry
year (July to June) by CF.

During scrutiny of records of 21 divisions, we noticed that in Betul West,
Guna and Indore Divisions, commercial rates of forest produce for the forestry
years 2008 to 20137 were fixed with a delay of one to seven months by the
respective CF. CF Shivpuri did not fix the commercial rates for the years
2008-09 and 2010-11. Therefore, the loss calculated in the cases of illicit
felling in these Divisions was not according to revised rates.

We further observed in Guna Division that commercial rates for other forest
produces (stone, sand and soil etc.) were fixed by CF, Shivpuri on per truck,
per trolley, per bullock cart and animal load basis instead of standard units.
The unit adopted for measurement of such other forest produce would not
exhibit correct quantity.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that all CFs were being instructed to fix the
commercial rates in prescribed time in each forestry year. The reply is,
however, silent about the units of measurement for other forest produce.

2.3.11.3 Lack of appropriate action in cases of encroachment

According to Section 80A of IFA, any person, who illegally possesses or
continues to possess forest land, may be ejected by orders of DFO from such
land without any adverse effect to the other actions such as imposing
penalty/prosecution under the Act. The deficiencies/shortcomings noticed in
the matter of dealing with the cases of encroachment were reported in the
CAG's Report (Revenue Receipt), GOMP 2002-03. However, the deficiencies
still persisted during the period 2008-13.

We noticed (between October 2012 and June 2013) that in the 21 test checked
Divisions, 10,710.220 ha of forest land was reported as encroached in 3,490
cases during the period 2008-2012

As per FOCR of Sawligarh Range and court case files of DFO, West Betul, 42
cases of encroachment of 21 ha of forest land (involving 119 offenders) in
Compartment No 1392 were registered during 2008-10. Though, the forest
land was vacated, no legal action under IFA was taken against the offenders
In the same compartment, 15 ha of forest land was again encroached in 2011
and 2012 by 84 offenders. Six cases were registered and the di
submitted five cases before the court of law against 83 offenders. Action such
as notice under Section 80(A) of IFA for ejection of encroachers was not
found on record.

2.3.1.4 Non-disposal of perishable property seized in forest offences

According to the section 58 of IFA, the Magistrate may direct sale of any
property seized under Section 52 of IFA which is subject to speedy and
material decay. The deficiency noticed in the issue of disposal of seized timber
was pointed out in the CAG’s report (Revenue Receipt) GOMP 2002-03.

We noticed (between October 2012 and June 2013) that in the depots of 117%
test checked divisions, 6,077 logs of teak/other species timber” (248.332
cu m) valued हैं 37.36 lakh seized in forest offences (September 1986 to May
2012) were lying undisposed as the Department did not apply to the Court for
order of disposal of the seized timber. Obviously, the delay in disposal of
timber would result in reduction in its value.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that seized forest produce under Iii
be disposed off after obtaining permission of the Courts.

The fact remains that prompt action is required for disposal as the material
was perishable in nature, hence the Department needed to take expeditious
permission from the Court.

2.3.11.5  Non- disposal of vehicles confiscated in forest offences

Government of MP, Forest Department, directed (January 1992) that an upset
price in respect of vehicles confiscated in forest offence should be fixed
through an officer-in-charge/PWD (O&M) and vehicles be disposed off
through public auction within four months from the date of confiscation.

We observed (between October 2012 and June 2013) in 18’7 out of 21
divisions that 456 vehicles’ were confiscated in forest offences during August
1993 to November 2012 (Appendix 2.25) but not disposed off within four
months from the date of confiscation and which were lying undisposed (March
2013). We observed that in seven” out of these 18 divisions, upset price of
171 confiscated vehicles had also not been fixed.

PCCF stated (September 2013) that in some cases vehicles could not be
disposed off due to non-receipt of bids and in other cases due to pendency of
cases in courts. The reply is not acceptable as cases pointed out in audit were
not pending in courts and confiscated material could have been dispased by
the department to avoid deterioration in value.

Effective control on forest offences depends upon regular monitoring of day-
to-day events of field. The monitoring mechanism of registration and disposal
of forest offence cases has been provided in the Acts. Accordingly,
instructions were issued for submission of copies of counterfoils of
preliminary offence reports: on line registration in online software FOMS,
maintenance of FOCR, beat books, range books, keeping timber account of
seized material, beat inspection etc.

The outcome of scrutiny of these mechanisms is discussed below
2.3.12.1 Inadequate monitoring of illegal mining

2000 and July 2004) to carry out joint inspection by the Mining Officer ar
officers nominated by District Collectors and 9705 for controlling illegal
mining in forest areas and to submit report to the Principal Secretary. Forest
Department (GOMP) every month through APCCF/Protection.

> We observed (July 2013) trom the records of Forest Department relating 10

for the entire period of 2008 to 2012 except by DFO, Indore in March
2013 after being pointed out by us. This indicated poor monitoring of the
Department over illegal mini

In the exit conference the Department accepted the observation and stated that

> It was further observed that Ministry of Environment and Forest (MoEF),
sanctioned (August 2002) land diversion of 100 ha for Northern Coalfields

Prevention of forest offences in the State was not adequate during 2008-12.
Number of encroachment cases doubled during the period and 51 per cent of
the wireless sets were not in working condition, affecting the prevention of
forest offences. The Department did not ensure use of the hired vehicles for
patrolling work.

Detection of forest offence was also deficient as satellite image obtained were
not analysed to undertake inspections for prompt registration of forest offence
cases. There was also shortfall in beat inspections.

There were also instances of delays in investigation for assessing loss of forest
produce and disposal of offence cases resulting in large number of
becoming time barred.

Monitoring of forest offence cases was inadequate and inspection reports for
controlling illegal mining were not being submitted to the Government.

The State Government may consider implementation of the following:
> Periodical review of the trend of various offences and framing of policy
for effective management and protection of forest.

> Optimisation of use of available infrastructure of Information Technology
for timely detection of forest offences and strengthening the system of
registration of cases of forest offences.

> Streamlining the system of valuation of loss and measurement of
confiscated timber and disposal of cases within time limit.

v

Monitoring mechanism of control systems related to forest offences be
strengthened.

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Executive summary

=a Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY), a Centrally Sponsored Scheme aims
at achieving four per cent annual growth in the agricultural sector during the
eleventh plan period, by ensuring holistic development of agriculture and
allied sectors. In the State , the projects under RKVY are being implemented
by Farmer Welfare & Agriculture Development Department (FW&AD), the
nodal department, and other departments such as, Veterinary, Horticulture.
Fisheries, Co-operative etc. In the State, 402 RKVY projects were executed
during the period 2007-13, of which, 297 projects had been completed.

The planning process for implementation of RKVY was deficient. The work
of preparation of District Agricultural Plans (DAPs) was awarded belatedly in
June 2009. The plans prepared for the years 2007-08 to
2010-11 were of no use due to delayed preparation of State Plans. The State
Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC) did not hold meetings as per the
required frequency and accorded post-facto approvals to the projects valued at
₹ 213.42 crore. SLSC should hold meeting regularly for sanction and
monitoring of the progress of approved projects. DAP should be prepared in
time by actively involving gram sabhas.

(Paragraphs 2.4.6.1, 2.4.6.2 & 2.4.6.3)

Financial management was not effective. FW&AD and Jawahar Lal Nehru
Krishi Vishwa Vidhyalaya (INKVV) utilised ₹ 8.75 crore for purposes other
than those for the Scheme. Animal Husbandry Department (AHD) parked
unspent balance of 13.94 crore for 2007-10 which remained unspent till the
end of year 2010-11 and हैं 1.45 crore till 2012-13 in Personal Deposit
Account, instead of remitting to FW&AD. Utilisation Certificates (UCs)
should be sent in the prescribed format giving all the requisite information.

In JNKVV, Jabalpur three research projects were abandoned thereby defeating

(Paragraph 2.4.8.1)

Programme management under RKVY was not satisfactory. In Custom
Hiring Centers (CHCs), tractors and implements were underutilised up to the
extent of 56 per cent of targets set. Four cattle breeding farms did not function
due to incomplete civil works. Cold chain for the purpose of preserving
potency of vaccines and for artificial insemination was not established in the
manner as planned. The scheme of CHCs should be adequately propagated to
ensure optimal utilisation of tractors.

No separate project-wise accounts/ledgers were maintained for accounting of
expenditure on projects under RKVY. FW&AD has not formed any dedicated
monitoring cell for evaluation and monitoring of performance of projects.
Project-wise accounts/ledgers should be maintained for accounting of receipts
and expenditure on project. The State Level Monitoring Cell (SLMC) should
be formed to ensure effective coordination and monitoring of project.

(Paragraphs 2.4.9 & 2.4.10)

With the aim of achieving four per cent annual growth in the agricultural

sector during the eleventh plan period by ensuring holistic development of

agriculture and allied sectors, the centrally Sponsored Scheme, RKVY, also

known as National Agriculture Development Programme (NADP) was

launched in May 2007. The main objectives of the scheme are to:

> incentivise States so as to increase public investment in agriculture and
allied sectors;

> provide flexibility and autonomy to States by the Central Government, in
the process of planning and executing agriculture and allied sector schemes
in the State and districts based on agro-climatic conditions, availability of
technology and natural resources; and

> ensure that local needs/crops/priorities are better reflected in agricultural
plans of States;

During the period 2007-08 to 2012-13, Government of India (GOT) released

₹ 1,900.79 crore for the Scheme, of which % 1,774.80 crore was spent. Out of

402 projects taken up under the scheme during 2007-13, 297 were completed.
81 were in progress and 24 were not implemented as of March 2013.

The average growth rate of Gross State Domestic Product under Agriculture
and allied Sector in the State, as per the Planning Commission (GOT), during
the period between 2008-09 and 2012-13 had been 9.83 per cent*' against the
national average of 2.88 per cent™ at 2004-05 prices. High growth was helped
by large allocation by the State Government over and above RKVY. State
Government increased® the Plan outlay under Agriculture and allied sector
from हैं 581.46 crore during 2007-08 to % 2,127.71 crore during 2012-13.
Under Agriculture and allied sector, State Government implements several
schemes, such as Surajdhara, Annapurna, Haldhar Yojna, etc. through State
budget. Besides, Central schemes, such as National Food Security Mission.
National Horticulture Mission, etc. are also undertaken under the sector.

*1 2008-09: 8.85 per cent, 2009-10: 9.78 per cent. 2010-11: (-) 0.14 per cent, 2011-12
17.31 per cent and 2012-13: 13.36 per cent.

% 2008-09: 0.09 per cent, 2009-10: 0.81 per cent, 2010-11: 7.94 per cent, 2011-12: 3.65
per cent and 2012-13: 1.91 per cent.

The State Agriculture Department (SAD) 1.०... FW&AD is the nodal
Department for execution of projects under RKVY. Apart from the nodal
Department the projects under the RKVY are also being implemented by other
departments such as, Horticulture and Food Processing (H&FP), Veterinary &
Animal Husbandry (AHD), Fisheries, Co-operative and two™ Agricultural
Universities. A SLSC constituted (November 2007) headed by the Chief
Secretary was to sanction, monitor the progress and review implementation of
projects under RKVY. The Government also constituted (July 2010) a
Committee, headed by the Agriculture Production Commissioner for monthly
review of implementation of RKVY projects.

The organogram depicting the organisational set up of the implementing
departments are given in Appendix 2.26.

The performance audit (PA) was conducted with a view to assess whether:

> Planning process for the implementation of RKVY was time bound.
effective and according to the Scheme guidelines;

> Financial management ensured adequate and timely availability of funds
and their effective and economic utilisation;

> Projects were implemented according to the regulatory structure in place
and the intended objectives of the projects were achieved; and

» Monitoring mechanism was adequate to ensure efficient and effective
implementation of the Scheme.

Under the PA, we selected 24 projects (including one incomplete) out of 297

‘An entry conference with the heads of concerned departments was held in

conference was held in January 2014 to discuss audit findings. Views
expressed during the conference have been suitably incorporated in the report

As per the guidelines, planning under the RKVY commences with the
preparation of DAP. The nodal Department prepares and finalises a
comprehensive State Agricultural Plan (SAP) by integrating DAPs. The
finalised SAP is submitted to the Department of Agriculture, GOT and
Planning Commission for approval. RKVY funds cannot be accessed without
approval of detailed project report (DPR) by SLSC.

24.6.1 Preparation of Comprehensive DAP/SAP

Para 4.1 of the guidelines provides that the components/activities which would
be admissible for project based assistance shall be adequately reflected in the
SAP and DAP. Further, as per the Manual for preparation of DAP, planning
process includes agriculture development plan from Gram Panchayat level to
district level. Thus, the effectiveness of the projects under RKVY depend on
the accuracy of DAP prepared by the District Agricultural Planning Unit
(DAPL) with the help of District Planning Team, stake holders, experts,
scientists, Non-Government Organisations, Self-Help Groups etc. We
observed that FW&AD awarded (June 2009) the work of preparation of
DAP for the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 to third parties (five agencies’).

The shortcomings noticed in preparation of DAP are discussed below:
Delayed preparation of DAP

The Ministry of Agriculture, GOT released (January 2008) one-time grant of
% 4.50 crore to GOMP during 2007-08 for preparation of DAP and SAP. The
Department, however, took more than one year to invite (January 2009)
Expression of Interest (Eol) for preparation of DAP. The work for 48
districts” for the Eleventh Plan period (2007-08 to 2011-12) was awarded
(June 2009) to five agencies, for completion during June-September 2009.

Team includes Chairman, Chief Executive Ofticer, Chiet’ Planning Officer of Zila
Panchayat. Joint Director/Deputy Director of Agriculture, Horticulture, Sericulture,
AHD, Fisheries and Industry and Commerce Departments and Dy. Conservator of!
Forest, Forest Department.

Agriculture, GOI to M/s MANAGE.

We observed that DAP trom 2007-08 to 2010-11 procured at a cost of & 1.94
crore™ became irrelevant as SAPs of these years were submitted to FW&AD
by the agency only in May 2011 as against the scheduled completion period of
September 2009.

The Director, FW&AD stated that DAP of 2007-08 and 2008-09 were
included in the scope of work on the basis of GOI instructions. In the exit
conference, the Principal Secretary stated that the delay was due to delayed
issue of guidelines for the Five Year Plan by the Central Planning Commission
(CPC).

The reply is not acceptable since, DAP manual, containing the guidelines, was
issued by the Planning Commission in March 2008. Besides, the Department
failed to ensure completion of the work within the stipulated period, due to
which even DAPs for 2009-10 and 2010-11 did not serve the purpose.

Non-involvement of Panchayats

As per para 3.3 of DAP manual issued by the Planning Commission in March
2008, the Gram Panchayat Plan was to be approved by the elected body of the
Gram Panchayat. The plans approved by Zila Panchyat were to be forwarded
to the State Government. We observed that out of 48 districts, in 39 districts”,
clearance of the Plan from Zila Panchayat was not found on record indicating
that grass root level agencies were not involved. Further, test check of DAP
for Dhar District” indicated that the agency took sample of only one village
each under 13 blocks for preparation of DAP instead of considering all the
villages, thus diluting the very purpose of preparation of DAP.

The Director. FW&AD stated (October 2013) that the Collectors, as Member
Secretary of District Planning Commission approved DAPs. The reply,
however, is silent about involving Gram Sabhas/Zila Panchayats in the
planning process of DAPs.

2.4.6.3 Ex-Post-facto approval of projects

The para 7.1.2 of guidelines for RKVY provide that no fund can be accessed
without approval of DPR by SISC headed by the Chief Secretary.

We noticed that eight projects were approved (2008-12) by SLSC at a cost of
% 105.62 crore. Due to excess expenditure of € 96.30 crore on these projects,
the revised cost of & 201.91 crore was sanctioned by the Chairman, SLSC
without holding meeting of SLSC.

Similarly, 23 new cases” (Appendix 2.28) were approved (2008-12) for
2 117.12 crore by the Chairman, SLSC in anticipation of the sanction by
SLSC, though the guidelines did not confer such authority to the Chairman,
SLSC. SLSC accorded ex post facto sanction of total amount of & 213.42
crore (% 96.30 crore +& 117.12 crore) in June 2012.

In the exit conference, the Director, FW&AD stated that all the cases where
ex-post-facto approval was given, expenditure was incurred with prior
approval of the Chairman, SLSC so as to utilise the funds which might be held
up due to not holding of SLSC meetings. The reply is not tenable as guidelines
provide for prior approval of SLSC and incurring expenditure with the
approval of Chairman, SLSC was not in line with the Scheme guidelines.

2.4.6.4 Functioning of State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC)

SLSC was constituted by the Government in October 2007. Para 6.4 of the
guidelines stipulates that SLSC should meet at least once in a quarter. As per
para 6.3, SLSC is entrusted with the responsibility of sanctioning, monitoring
and reviewing projects under RKVY.

We, however, observed that during the period 2007-08 to 2011-12, against
stipulated 20 meetings, only nine meetings of SLSC were held which included
one meeting each during 2007-08 and 2008-09. Thus, sanctioning, monitoring
and reviewing process of projects done under RKVY stipulated in the
guidelines was deficient.

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary agreed to the necessity for
increasing the number of meetings.

2.4.6.5 Establishment of State Level Monitoring Cell

Para 5.1 of the guidelines stipulate that SAD shall be the nodal department for
implementation of the Scheme. For administrative convenience and case of
implementation, the State Government may identify or create an exclusive
agency for implementing the Scheme on fast track.

crore was lying in PD account at the end of the year 2012-13, which was not
surrendered to the nodal department. Thus, the funds for RKVY remained idle
during these periods.

and had to be surrendered in October 2011 as per the decision taken by review
meeting headed by Assistant Agriculture Production Commissioner. PD
Account was being operated with the permission of the Finance Department.

crore would be utilised before March 2014 in purchasing and installation of
X-ray and Sonography machines.

The fact remains that the amounts remained idle with AHD and the balance
amount of 2012-13 was yet to be used.

used by CHCs under the control of DAE for the year 2011-12. Against this,
the average utilisation hy CHCs was 292 hours per tractor. Far the year
2012-13, DAE, reduced the targets to 700 hours per tractor. However,
achievement during 2012-13 was only 526 hours per tractor. Even
considering the reduced targets, there was shortfall in use of tractors, in
respect of CHCs. Thus, the objective of promoting farm mechanisation was
not fully achieved.

> In five Department/organisations'”* working hours achieved with reference
to targeted hours in respect of 774 tractors (2011-12) and 564 tractors
(2012-13)' indicated that there were shortfalls ranging from 20 per cent
to 50 per cent during 2011-12 and 21 per cenl to 56 per cent during
2012-13 when compared with the actual achievement of CHCs controlled
by DAE as detailed in Appendix 2.30. The shortfall in utilisation was
highest in CHCs run by MARKFED (50 per cent) and Co-operative
department (55.65 per cent) during the year 2011-12 and 2012-13,
respectively.

In reply, DAE stated that Scheme is demand driven, based on socio-
economic and agro-climatic conditions instead of target-achievement
based. Usage of assets was subject to conditions such as awareness among
farmers, economic feasibility and availability of other alternate methods.
Further, the targets were fixed on higher side to put pressure for
maximising the use of tractors.

The reply confirms that the requirement of establishment of CHCs and
fixing of target was determined without assessing the actual demand.

> During the period 2011-2013, the MP Beej Nigam was provided 44
tractors (valued at हैं 3.90 crore) for use of the same by the farmers of
CHCs adjacent to farms of the Nigam. We observed that the tractors were
used by the farmers of CHCs as well as in the farms of the Nigam. As per
the information on working hours of tractors provided by the Beej Nigam
for 44 tractors, 11 tractors were not at all used. For remaining 33 tractors,
the average use (for farmers of CHCs) was only 112 hours during this
period against 218 hours for its own farm. Therefore, while the tractors
were for use of farmers they were largely unutilised or were for used by
the Beej Nigam itself, thus, defeating the purpose of the project.

In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that the main reason
for under performance was lack of drivers. The performance of CHCs
indicates that the Scheme was not well conceived and planned in advance
to overcome the bottlenecks in implementation of the Scheme to provide
tractors on hire to the needy farmers at an affordable price.

(iii) Monitoring of CHCs by Jila Mechanisation Committee

As per the guidelines for establishing of CHCs issued (24 February 2011) by

the Government. monitoring of CHCs at district level was to be done by the

Jila Mechanisation Committee (IMC) headed by the District Collector. The

Agriculture Engineer/Assistant Agriculture Engineer of DAE was designated

as the member Secretary of JMC. Test check of records and information

relating to JMCs meetings of 18 districts'' revealed the following:

> The meeting of JMC for the purpose of monitoring was to be held twice in
a year in October and April. We observed that meetings of JMC were
either not held or held only once during the year 2011-12 and 2012-13
indicating lack of consistent monitoring of implementation of project in the
districts!"

> The rate for hiring charges for all CHCs under Shajapur, Indore, Mandsaur
and Dhar Districts under Indore Division was increased during 2011-12
without any analysis of actual cost.

> A review of minutes of meetings of JMCs revealed that Member-Secretary
did not monitor the overall performance/working of CHCs so as to analyse
reasons for shortfall in performance and take remedial action. The
guidelines issued for CHCs do not specify the duties/ responsibilities of
Member-Secretary of IMCs.
The Government (January 2014) stated that directives had been issued
(December 2013) to JMCs for detailed discussion on change of hiring
charges, holding of meetings at least twice in a year and powers and duties
of the Member-Secretary had been further elaborated.

2.4.8.5 Integrated Pest Management (IPM) Sector

Crop protection

IPM technique recognises the importance of insecticides to be judiciously used
against pests by pest surveillance, use of biological agents and bio-pesticides
for the purpose of plant protection. SLSC approved (May 2010) a project of
“Crop protection (2010-11)" for promotion of bio pesticide developed from
plants and plant extracts. The proposal was submitted to SLSC for covering an
area of 40,000 ha at हैं 750 per ha (cost: % 3 crore), though no DPR was
prepared. SLSC sanctioned & 8 crore for covering of 40,000 ha, though actual
cost of covering 40,000 ha worked out % 3 crore. This indicates that the
approvals accorded hy SLSC were without detailed examination of the
proposals sent by the departments.

We observed that FW&AD distributed & 8 crore to 37 districts covering an
area of 1,06,666 ha. Instead of remitting back the allotment in excess of
requirement for coverage of 40000 ha, the coverage of area for crop protection
project was increased without obtaining approval of SLSC, which was
irregular. As of March 2013, 97,917 ha had been covered.

On this being pointed out, FW&AD replied (July 2013) that due to typing
error the area was wrongly written as 40,000 ha instead of 1,06,666 ha.

The reply is not acceptable as the proposal submitted for approval indicated
coverage of 40,000 ha at two places and typing mistakes do not occur in such
manner.

(ii) Execution of work through MARKFED

MARKFED, registered under Madhya Pradesh Co-operative Societies Act,
1960, is an Apex body of Co-operative Societies to facilitate, coordinate and
promote the marketing and trading activities of the cooperative institutions in
agricultural commodities. Projects under RKVY such as, strengthening of
block offices as Kisan Gyan Kendra, strengthening of SIAET and SLMC,
were entrusted to MARKFED by FW&AD,

Our scrutiny of records relating to award of works to MARKFED revealed the
following shortcomings:

> As per the rule 153, chapter 8 of M.-P Financial Code, all original works
under the administrative control of other departments valued at हैं. 2500
and above are to be executed through Public Works Department (PWD).
In special cases where the works are not to be executed through PWD,
prior consultation shall be held with the Accountant General before
engaging contractor; so that suitable provisions for its audit and financial
control could be made. We observed that FW&AD awarded works under
above stated three projects costing = 32.44 crore to MARKFED instead of
PWD. FW&AD also did not consult the Accountant General before
awarding the works though envisaged in the M P Financial Code.

As per the Government orders (October 2010), the percentage charges for
consultancy, supervision. quality control and contingency is limited to six
per cent of cost for building work if executed by PWD. We observed that
MARKFED charged 7.5 per cent as supervision consultancy (excluding
architectural consultancy of five per cent) against six per cent rate
charged by PWD. Thus, assigning of these three works to MARKFED has
resulted in avoidable extra cost of € 0.96 crore.

» The work of “Renovation and additional work at the Administrative
Building and Laboratory Building of SIAET” under the project of
“Strengthening of SIAET” estimated at % 89 lakh was awarded (June
2008) by MARKFED to a contractor at the quoted price of € 1.32 crore!!®,
The final bill of हैं 1.63 crore was paid in May 2009. The payment
included an amount of हैं 50.53 lakh for execution of such items which
were not in the bill of quantity and the schedule of rates (non-SOR). The

गत. Contractor quoted at 47.99 per cent above the estimated cost of हैं 89.00 lakh based on

PWD SOR for buildings.

2.4.8.7 Integrated development of 60,000 villages for pulse crops in rain fed
areas

The GOT issued (March 2011) guidelines for integrated development of
60,000 villages of pulses crops in rain fed areas in India during 2011-12. The
project included in-situ moisture conservation, accelerated pulses production
programme (A3P) with inclusion of mini kits and pest surveillance and market
linked extension support.

As per the guidelines for the project, preference was to be given to the areas
with no assured irtigation source. The project for हैं 55.48 crore under RKVY
for the year 2011-12, was approved by SLSC in July 2011. Against the
sanction, the Department incurred an expenditure of हैं 55.48 crore on the
project in 29 districts. Our scrutiny of the process of selection of
villages/districts under the scheme indicated following:

(i Incorrect selection of areas

As per para 2.4 of the guidelines, implementation of the programme
implementation was to be watershed centric’ and that the water shed villages
not covered under the previous year was to be taken up under the project. We.
however, noticed that FW&AD selected 29 districts without specifying the
villages. although the scheme required selection of specific villages to be
covered. We also observed that FW&AD could not provide details of the
watershed villages covered during previous year. Thus, the project was
approved by SLSC without requisite details in respect of districts/villages so
as to prioritise them according to the need. In the absence of such details, we
could not check whether the selected villages were actually needed for the
project. In three districts'** comprising of 333 villages under 11 blocks
covered under the project, all the villages were having assured irrigation
source such as, river, tube wells and wells. Thus, selection of such villages
was not according to the guidelines for the project.

(ii) Prioritisation of districts

The study report of the National Rainfed Area Authority, Planning
Commission, GOI published in February 2012 prioritised 499 rain fed
districts'*’ of India including 50 districts in Madhya Pradesh based on natural
resources and livelihood status as per Rainfed Areas Prioritisation Index
