establishment of monitoring cell. The Government also stated (January 2014)
that, format of the ledger would shortly be sent to other stakeholders,
departments for keeping project-wise accounting and instructions had also
been issued.

Monitoring of the Scheme ensures that activities are carried out according to

the prescribed rules, regulations and that the projects are executed as per the

sanction accorded. As per the revised order for formation of SLSC issued

(November 2007) by the Government, SLSC was to review the achievement

of objectives, progress of work and implementation of projects.

The deficiencies in monitoring noticed during audit are as under:

> FW&AD has not formed any dedicated monitoring cell for evaluation and
monitoring of performance of projects and ensuring co-ordination with
other departments for effective implementation of the projects.

> As per para 6.3 of guidelines, SLSC was entrusted with the responsibility
to monitor the progress of the sanctioned projects/schemes. However, the
scrutiny of the agenda meetings of SLSC, revealed that the progress of
sanctioned projects were not being monitored by SLSC.

> The scrutiny of project proposals and minutes of SLSC meetings revealed
that the projects were approved by SLSC without specilying a definite
time limit for its completion. Thus, there was no completion dates to
monitor the progress of the projects. There were instances of inordinate
delay in completion of projects.

> No mechanism was in place in FW & AD to monitor the progress of
projects executed on deposit basis through MARKFED.

The Comprehensive DAP were prepared without involving gram sabhas.
DAPs prepared for 2007-11 were irrelevant due to delayed preparation of
SAP. SLSC did not hold meetings as per the required frequency for approval
and monitoring of the projects and accorded posi-facto approvals to the
projects. Thus, planning process for project preparation was weak.

Instances of fund lying unutilised, submission of UCs without requisite
information and without incurring expenditure and utilisation of funds for the
purposes other than for RKVY indicated deticient financial management.

We observed deficiencies in implementation of RKVY projects. The research
projects taken up by INKVV were not completed. Utilisation of tractors by
the CHCs was far below the specificd utilisation hours, which affected optimal
performance of CHCs. Cattle breeding farms were yet to start function due to
incomplete civil works. Cold-chain was not established in the manner as
planned. Under integrated development of pulses villages scheme, areas

89


Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

having assured irrigation source were also selected in violation of the scheme
guidelines.

Monitoring mechanism was not effective since the Department did not have a
separate cell for this purpose. Besides, separate project-wise accounts/ledgers
were not specitied/ maintained for accounting of expenditure on projects.

> SLSC should ensure timely and regular holding of meetings and monitor
the progress of approved projects. DAP should be prepared in time by
actively involving gram sabhas.

> SLMC should be formed to ensure effective coordination and monitoring
of projects.

> UCs should give all the requisite information as per the prescribed format.

» Projects should be approved with a definite time frame for its completion
with all its components to ensure desired benefits. The scheme of CHCs
should be adequately propagated to ensure optimal utilisation of tractors.

> Since cold-chain involves availability of cooling device at every stage of
storage/ distribution, availability of equipment at every stage of cold chain
is required for cold-chain to be effective.

> Project-wise accounts/ledgers should be maintained for accounting of
receipts and expenditure on projects.

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

Executive summary

A three tier quality management mechanism has been operationalized under
PMGSY for ensuring that quality of assets created contorms to the prescribed
standards. A Performance Audit on Quality Assurance in construction of rural
roads by MPRRDA during the period 2008-09 to 2012-13 revealed various
deficiencies, the major issues are as under:

There was lack of quality assurance for preparation of Detailed Project
Reports (DPRs) as data regarding design traffic, its growth rate and possible
future changes in the road was not ensured for preparation of DPRs leading to
deterioration of roads. Government should ensure preparation of DPRs based
on adequate survey and investigation as per Indian Roads Congress
(IRC)/Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD) specifications.

(Paragraph 2.5.4.1)
There were shortages of testing equipment in district laboratories required for
project preparation and quality control resulting in lack of quality assurance in
execution of road works. Government should ensure that all required
equipment are made available in disirict level laboratories for conduct of the
required tests at every stage.

(Paragraph 2.5.4.2)
Quality in respect of execution of road works was not assured as prescribed
tests for alignment, regularity and surface levels, earthwork, granular work.
bituminous work and concrete work were not carried out and adequately
recorded. Government should ensure conduct of adequate quality tests for
road works as per required frequency.

(Paragraph 2.5.4.3 to 2.5.4.9)

Function of second tier quality control mechanism was deficient as frequency
of inspection by State Quality Monitors (SQMs) was inadequate and Project
Implementation Units (PTUs) did not report compliance with SQM suggestions
to the State Quality Coordinator (SQC). Government should strengthen
functioning of SOM and ensure adherence to the corrective suggestions made
them.

(Paragraph 2.5.5.1)
There was lack of monitoring quality control activities, as reflected from
shortfall in inspections of sites by Assistant Engineers (AEs)/Executive
Engineers (EEs), non-submission of the monthly returns of tests by AEs to
EEs. Government should ensure that inspection works are carried out as per
the required frequency by the departmental officers and SOM.

(Paragraph 2.5.6.1 to 2.5.6.4)



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

As per Road Statistics (2011-12) published by Ministry of Road Transport and
Highways, in Madhya Pradesh State, the length of rural roads covers 96,611
km!” (48 per cent) of 2,01,261 km total road length in the State. Rural roads
are mainly constructed under PMGSY through MPRRDA, a registered
society. Up to 31 March 2012, roads of 49.445 km (51 per cent) were
constructed by MPRRDA. The rural roads constructed under PMGSY provide
connectivity hy way of all-weather road (with necessary culverts and cross-
drainage structures, which are operable throughout the year) to the eligible
unconnected habitations in the rural areas.

The quality control in the context of road works aims at reducing the chance of
premature failure or damage of roads as well as construction of good riding
quality roads. A three tier quality management mechanism has been
operationalised under PMGSY for ensuring that quality of assets created
conforms to the prescribed standards.

The first tier quality management system in MPRRDA, is implemented
through the Chief Engineers (CEs), Superintending Engineers (SEs)
designated as Chief General Managers (CGMs) at circle levels and by EEs
designated as General Managers (GMs) at PIU for the 50 districts in the State.
PTUs are responsible for preparation of realistic DPRs, besides ensuring that
contractors have brought the necessary machinery and equipment to site and
key engineering personnel have been deployed by the contractor.

The second tier quality management is undertaken centrally by MPRRDA
through its Quality Control Cell which acts as the SQC'*" by coordinating with
the independent consultants appointed by MPRRDA as SQMs. It is the
responsibility of the State Government to operationalise the first and the
second tiers of the quality management structure. State Technical Agency’!
scrutinises and countersigns DPRs for ensuring its correctness before sending
proposal to National Rural Road Development Agency (NRRDA). Monitoring
by independent National Quality Monitors (NQMs)'™ constitutes the third tier
of this arrangement. In the State, NQMs conducted 982 inspections between
November 2010 and February 2014.

Funds for construction of rural roads under PMGSY are provided by GOI and
are supplemented, since 2004-05, by loan assistance from Asian Development

Constructed by Local SelfGovernment _Department/State Panchyati Raj
Department/State Rural Road Departments and Nation Rural Road Development
Ageney, Ministry of Rural Road excluding JRY roads.

SQC, 2 wing of MPRRDA is responsible to oversee the system of quality controls
deployed by MPRRDA.

There are three State (echnical agencies in the State situated at Bhopal, Indore and
Jabalpur.

Individual monitor is appointed by NRRDA, who independently examines the quality
control of rural roads

Bank and State budgetary support. The funds are distributed to PIUs for

The audit was conducted to assess the adequacy and efficiency in ensuring
quality controls in planning and execution of rural roads under PMGSY by
PIUs of MPRRDA and quality monitoring by PIUs and SQMs.

Audit criteria adopted were guidelines on quality assurance and control
mechanism, Quality Assurance Handbook (QAHB) for Rural Roads with
reference to norms, specifications and provisions of Specifications of Rural
Roads published by MORD and Specifications of IRC for rural roads.

Records relating to quality control in construction of PMGSY roads by
MPRRDA for the period 2008-09 to 2012-13 were test checked in audit during
November 2012 to August 2013. Out of the 50 districts, audit selected 17
districts (34 per cent) on random sampling basis and undertook detailed
examination of 1,158 of the 3,058 road works completed in the selected
districts. Scope of audit scrutiny was limited to first tier and second tier quality
management by the State Government.

An entry conference with the Chief Executive Officer (CEO) of MPRRDA
was held in December 2012 to discuss objectives, scope and methodology of
Audit. Exit conference was also held in September 2013 with CEO to discuss
audit findings. Views of MPRRDA expressed in the exit conference have been
suitably incorporated in the Report.

MPRRDA engaged quality supervision consultants for assurance of quality
controls. As per contract conditions, the consultants are required to administer
the services as ‘Engineer’ for ensuring that the contractual clauses related to
the quality and quantity are adhered to and the works are implemented in
accordance with the provisions/specifications.

Deficiencies in adherence to prescribed quality control tests and procedures in
QAHB noticed in examination of 57 road works of 10 PIUs, have been
discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.5.4.1 Unrealistic estimates of future traffic in Detailed Project Report
(DPR)

Para 3.1 (i) of Chapter 2 of QAHB for rural roads provides guidelines for
preparation of realistic DPRs for planning and construction of rural roads.

Para 5.2.2 of IRC: SP-20 envisages obtaining a realistic estimate of design
traffic, due consideration of the existing traffic, its growth rate, possible
change in the road network and land use of the area.

We noticed in case of five roads of the test checked 10 PIUs that DPRs were
prepared on the basis of unrealistic assessment of future traffic leading to
inadequate provision of crust resulting in deterioration of roads as discussed
below:

> — In Bhopal PIU, Bagmugalia to Barrai road was sanctioned on the basis
of prevailing traffic load in the year
2008-09 and completed in January
2011. In March 2011, it came to the
notice of MPRRDA that the road
condition deteriorated due to heavy
traffic of the nearby colonies being
constructed (started in September
2010) by the Bhopal Development
Authority (BDA). We observed that
construction of the colonies
View of deteriorated crust of Bagmugalia to (Bagmugalia and Barrai villages)
Rarrai road due to non assessment of future traffic was already included in the Bhopal
and insufficient provision in DPR. (Status! Development Plan (BDP) 2005.
September 2013) Failure to consider the increase in
future traffic due to growth envisaged in BDP led to premature deterioration of
the crust of the road. GM therefore sent (March 2011) a proposal for richer
crust on the basis of new traffic survey. Assessment of future traffic ignoring
increase in traffic due to construction of colony was not according to the
provisions of IRC: SP-20 and had led to premature deterioration of the road.

Regarding inadequate provision of crust, the Government stated (November
2013) that the road was constructed as per the specifications atter sanction by
NRRDA and as BDA had started the housing project when the work of the
road was complete, heavy traffic more than its capacity was passing through
the road resulting in damage to the road.

traffic due to construction of the said road was not considered while
assessing the projected traffic as details of assessment of generated traffic
due to construction of road were not found on records. The road was
subsequently damaged (December 2010) duc to heavy traffic.

In reply, it was stated (November 2013) that the roads were damaged
prematurely due to unexpected increase in traffic. Reply was however
silent as to why all the factors as prescribed in IRC: SP 20 were not
considered to assess the future traffic growth on the roads, which resulted
in premature failure.

2.5.4.2 Availability of equipment in district level laboratories

Section 100 of QAHB (Vol II) provides that the tests which are required to be
done during the project preparation stage such as tests for suitability of
construction material, selection of quarries etc. or the tests which are required
only once for quality control shall be conducted in district laboratory and
district laboratory will cover the testing requirements on a district level

We noticed shortages of important testing equipment viz. standard Proctor
Density Test apparatus for testing of California Bearing Ratio (CBR) value,
electrically operated centrifuge type bitumen extractor for bitumen content
test, Penetration Test apparatus for test of bitumen, Compression testing
machine for compressive strength test of concrete and aggregate Impact Value
test apparatus for aggregate impact test at district laboratories of five PIUs'**
as detailed in Appendix 2.34. Hoshangabad, Ratlam and Sehore did not have
four out of five equipments. In Chindwara, Harda and Vidisha PIUs, details of
apparatuses for various tests available with PIUs were not made available to
Audit during January 2013 to August 2013. Non-availability of equipment in
district level laboratories affected the quality control during the project

2.5.44 Quality assurance in earthwork of rural roads

As per para 6.1 of Section 300 of Quality Control Handbook (QCHB),
Standard Proctor Compaction Test (IS: 2720 Part 7) is conducted to ascertain
‘dry density to moisture content’ relationship in soil. Section 300 of the
MORD, GOI specifications provide that soil should be spread in successive
layers of uniform thickness over the entire width of the embankment'*! and
compaction of soil shall be carried out at a moisture content within (+) two per
cent of the optimum moisture content (OMC) for achieving maximum dry
density (MDD).

In the road works of six test checked PIUs, we observed that tests of natural
moisture content were not carried out (23 roads) and test of moisture content
of soil required to be carried out at the time of compaction were not mentioned
in QCRs (15 roads). In case of 12 road works of three PIUs, moisture content
obtained at time of compaction was lower than optimum moisture content"?
leading to non-achievement of MDD. Thus, achievement of MDD of soil was
not ensured, Details are given in Appendix 2.36.

The Government stated (November 2013) that required tests were conducted
by quality consultants and test checked by PIU staff and record of such tests
was also maintained by the consultants and handed over to PIU after
completion of works. Regarding achievement of MDD, the Government stated
that moisture content may vary as determined in lab and that in field by rapid
moisture meter.

The Department, however, did not provide any document evidencing conduct
of the tests though required in terms of QCHB in respect of the works pointed
out in audit. Further, PIU, Vidisha in January 2014, produced copies of test
reports to us, which were without any dates and without verification certificate
of GM. Besides, Government view regarding achievement of MDD is not
acceptable since MDD could only be achieved when moisture was within (+) 2
per cent in OMC.

2.5.4.5 Adequacy of thickness of selected soil in execution of road works

As per para 303 of QAHB, subgrade is top 300 mm compacted layer in
embankment with selected soil (CBR value 7 as provided in DPRs of road
works) and the subgrade in embankment is compacted to a higher standard (at
100 per cent Standard Proctor Density) than the lower layers of the
embankment.

We noticed however that in respect of 10 road works!” of upgradation in PIU,
Harda and PIU, Indore, the crust for pavement was designed and approved by
STA. As per DPR, the thickness of subgrade was approved for 150 mm
assuming availability of uniform thickness of 150 mm of subgrade of the
existing roads without conducting any test for ensuring available thickness and
quality of the existing crust. We further noticed that before executing the
subgrade of 150 mm, embankment was also executed with borrowed area soil
and material of unspecified CBR value obtained from roadway cutting. Thus,
requirement of top 300 mm of subgrade of CBR value seven was not ensured
in the upgradation of these roads.

The Government stated (November 2013) that roads under PIU Harda were
for upgradation of existing roads having crust in variable thickness, which was
considered as sub grade and the balance subgrade in variable thickness was
made up with select soil of CBR value 7 to achieve the designed thickness of
300 mm. In case of Indore PIU, it was stated that the new IRC SP:72'4 was
introduced and the crust was redesigned as per the provisions of SP:72. IL was
also stated that technical sanction was accorded for provision of subgrade in
150 mm thickness and the work was executed accordingly.

Availability of existing crust in variable thickness in different reaches of soil
having CBR value not less than seven was not mentioned in DPRs. Provision
of selected soil in thickness of 150 mm was made for entire length of the
roads, without ensuring availability of uniform thickness of 150 mm existing
crust having CBR value not less than seven, was not in accordance with the
provisions of QAHB (para 303), which provides for top 300 mm layer
subgrade of approved soil with CBR more than seven.

Non-Conformance Reports (NCRs) of grading of aggregate with reference

The Government stated (November 2013) that all the tests mentioned in

and regarding thickness of layer test QCRs would be updated. As regards

The reply is not acceptable as tests of bitumen were required to be carried out
as supply document did not carry all the quality parameters as required under
the provisions of QAHB. Regarding gradation of aggregate for OGPC, reply
is not correct as test results clearly indicated use of higher sized metal in
excess of norms.

Thus, due to not conducting required tests for bituminous works and not
taking remedial action in cases where tests results indicated deviations from
the norms, adherence to the required parameters of bituminous works was not
ensured in 24 works.

2.5.4.8 Inadequate quality control in execution of cement concrete work

As per para 8.9.3 of IRC SP:20 -2002, M-30 grade cement conerete (CC) is
applied in rural roads for which the mix proportion is 1:1.5:3 (cement, sand
and aggregate). Weigh batcher'®! is used for preparing mix of concrete by
weight as per mix design!°?. Further, mix design reports are prepared by
consultants and approved by GMs for use in CC works describing specific
ratio of ingredients by weight.

We noticed that in 56 road works of 23 packages in eight PIUs, ratio of mix
design in CC work was not as per approved mix design ratio or ratio of 1:1.5:3
prescribed in IRC SP:20. While approval of mix designs in respect of 21
roads was not on records, the prescribed ratio of 1:1.5:3 concrete was not
followed. In case of 36 road works, where approved mix designs were
available, details of use of weigh batcher during execution was not on record.
Evidently, because of non-use of weigh batcher, ratio of CC by weight could
not be ensured during construction as required in mix designs. The details are
shown in Appendix 2.37.

The Government stated (November 2013) that the work of CC was executed
as per approved mix design with weigh batcher and the quality of CC work
conforms to required strength of M-30 cement concrete.

The reply is not tenable as records maintained in PIUs did not indicate
approval of mix designs and use of weigh batchers in CC works. In
subsequent verification (January 2014), when mix designs reports were
produced by PIU Vidisha, we noticed that the mix designs were not approved
by competent authority.

2.5.4.9 Construction of road side drains for quality assurance

As per guidelines issued by NRRDA, while preparing DPRs, it is absolutely
essential to provide road side drains with suitable lining to collect the surlace
water from the roadways and also to drain the hase of the road way to prevent
saturation and loss of support for traffic.

We noticed in nine road works'** in PIU Vidisha that the side drains were not

constructed with lining as provided in DPRs.

The Government stated (November 2013) that the CC drains work was
executed where the land was available in village portion and the availability of
sufficient land was rarely guaranteed, as some times local people/land donors
changed their mind during construction stage.

The reply is not acceptable as CC work was not executed even where
excavation for drains was done as per the deviation statement for the package.
Besides, availability of sufficient land should have been ensured before
starting execution of the road work as construction of side drain was essential
to prevent saturation and consequential deterioration of road in terms of
NRRDA guidelines.

2.5.5.1 Monitoring by State Quality Monitors

QAHB prescribes to operationalise the second tier quality management
mechanism for quality monitoring through independent SQMs engaged by
MPRRDA. Function of the second tier of independent quality management is
to ensure that the Quality Management System at the site is functioning
satisfactorily and suggest possible improvements, wherever required. As per
guidelines on quality assurance and control mechanism issued by MORD,
GOI, the programme for SQMs should be drawn up in such a way that every
work is inspected at least three limes. PMGSY guidelines further provide for a
State Quality Control Coordinator (SQC) for preparing report on the
performance of State Quality Monitoring System. SQMs in MPRRDA are
engaged to oversee the quality of works independent from the executing
agency and to report to 50९.

Scrutiny of information made available by MPRRDA in respect of 17 PIUs
reviewed by Audit relating to quality inspection by SQMs during November
2010 to December 2013 to audit revealed following:

> During the period from November 2010 to January 2014, 2,035
inspections of 1,158 roads were done by SQMs in 17 districts. We
observed that while inspections were carried out as per prescribed norms
in respect of 205 roads, only 1,244 inspections were undertaken for the
remaining 953 roads against 2,859 inspections (953x3) due. Thus, there
was shortfall of 1,615 inspections (56.49 per cent) indicating inadequate
monitoring over quality of works executed.

> We observed that in 14 cases of inspection (2010-13) of completed roads,
in six test checked districts. inspections by SQMs were done after 37 days

their reports was ensured by MPRRDA. It was further stated that as per
QAHB for rural roads, function of second tier of independent quality
management was ensured and the Quality Management System at the site was
functioning satisfactorily.

The reply is not acceptable as there was shortfall in the inspection by SQM,
failure to report systemic flaws and compliance reports on inspections were
not being obtained in regular manner as brought out above.

2.5.6.1 Ensuring deployment of technical staff by the contractors

As per clause 9.1 of General Conditions of Contract, contractor shall employ
technical personnel as named in the contract agreement or technical person
approved by the Engineer for construction work and routine maintenance.

We observed that list of technical personnel deployed as per agreement, were
submitted by the contractors at the beginning of work execution. We also
observed instances of penalty levied from the contractors for non-deployment
of adequate technical staff. However, there was no system of continuous
monitoring of availability of required technical staff for entire period of
execution of work as prescribed in para 9.4 of operations manual in 47 of the
137 test checked road works in seven PIUs.

The Government stated (November 2013) that MPRRDA engaged supervision
and quality control consultants for supervision. Contractors had deployed staff
as per requirement of agreement. In case of failure to follow the provisions of
agreements GMs of PIUs were being instructed to initiate appropriate action
for levy of penalty etc. as per agreements.

However in the exit conference, it was admitted that MPRRDA was suffering
from the problem of deployment of technical staff by the contractors.

Engineers

As per para 3.1 (iv), Chapter-2 of QAHB, the frequency of inspection of site is
twice a week for AE and once a week for EE of the respective PIU during the
work in progress.

Control tests are being performed at the desired frequency and with the desired
accuracy. Payment to the contractor shall be regulated by EE as per the reports

We observed the following:

>  Insix PIUs'*, no monthly return for the tests conducted was submitted by

AEs to EEs to keep watch over conduct of tests and its frequency.

In the absence of monthly returns, frequency and accuracy of tests could not

it may not be possible for EEs to regulate payment to the contractors in
accordance with the NRRDA guidelines.

2.5.6.4 Monitoring by Superintending Engineers and Chief Engineer

As per para 11.4.5 of Operations Manual, SE and CE are responsible for
inspection and quality testing supervision of PIUs under their jurisdiction.
Accordingly, SEs and CE were to oversee the quality control functions and
testing procedure and to record the observations in QCRs during inspections.
State Government was to fix frequency of such inspections. Test check of
records of 10 PIUs revealed that though the inspections were done by SE and
CE, the frequency of inspection was not fixed by the State in this regard.

Preparation of DPRs for rural roads was not accurate due to injudicious
assessment of future traffic. There was absence of equipment in district level
laboratories for testing of material. Quality controls during various stages of
planning and execution of road works were not ensured since prescribed tests
were not carried out and recorded. PIUs did not ensure availability of
approved mix design of CC for works and use of weigh batcher. There was
shortfall in inspection of works by AEs/EEs. Second tier quality control was
deficient as SQMs were not inspecting all the rural roads or inspecting only
one stage of construction. Besides, compliance to reports of SQM was also
delayed and pending in many cases.

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

Executive Summary

The Water Resources Department (WRD) is responsible for planning.
estimation, execution, operation and maintenance of irrigation projects, safety
of dams, matters relating to users associations etc. The Department had
completed 13 major projects, 110 medium projects and 5,986 minor schemes
and there were seven major projects, 18 medium projects and 663 minor
ongoing schemes at various stages of construction at the end of March 20137.

Audit reviewed the functioning of the internal control mechanism in WRD
during the period 2010-13. We observed deficiencies in budgets, financial
operation and organisation controls in the Department:

During the period 2010-13, the Department did not surrender the entire
savings. Significant amounts were surrendered in the last week of the year
2010-11 (₹ 27.59 crore) and 2011-12 (₹ 162.32 crore). There was large
expenditure (23 to 36 per cent) in the month of March every year; in respect of
seven schemes it ranged from 75 per cent to 100 per cent. The Central
assistance ( 397.32 crore) was utilised in subsequent years indicating that the
money was not required for immediate disbursement. Government should
ensure that funds are drawn from treasury only for immediate use, the budgets
are prepared realistically to avoid savings and all savings should be
surrendered on time.

(Paragraphs 2.6.5.1 to 2.6.5.3)
Revised Administrative Approvals (AAs) regularising the excess expenditure
of & 83.95 crore in respect of 48 works was not obtained as of March 2013.
Reconciliation of cheques and remittance by the divisional officers with
treasury and submission of reconciliation statement to Accountant General
(Accounts & Entitlement) office delayed up to 157 months. Reconciliation of
remittances and drawals should be done by the Department on regular basis.

(Paragraphs 2.6.5.4 & 2.6.5.5)
Miscellaneous Works Advance against officials, contractors, and other
departments etc. were outstanding for long period. The advances of = 1257.66
crore paid to Land Acquisition Officers (LAOs) for acquisition of land for
irrigation projects was directly charged to final head of account instead of
debiting to the suspense account.

(Paragraph 2.6.5.7)

There were delays up to 57 months in remittances of Labour Welfare Cess

@ 5.90 crore) deducted from the contractors’ bills to Labour Welfare Board

thereby becoming liable to pay penalty. Department should ensure that Labour
Welfare Cess iy deposited with the Labour Welfare Board on time.

(Paragraph 2.6.5.1)

Irrigation projects are classified as major, medium and minor when the cultivable
command areas are more than 10,000 hectares. over 2,000 hectares and up to 10,000
hectares and 2,000 hectares or less, respectively.

107


Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Material at site accounts, contractor’s ledger and works abstract were not
maintained to ensure control over the receipt/issue of material for works and
transactions relating to advance payments, secured advances. adjustment of
advances and value of work done.

(Paragraphs 2.6.6.1 and 2.6.6.4)
Establishment for quality control functions was inadequate as only two main
laboratories were established though it should have been established at all the
seven major and 18 medium projects, field laboratories were not established at
any of the work sites. Besides, there were significant shortages in men in
position for many posts. Sanctioned strength and men in position of different
cadres should be reviewed and is suitably rationalised.

(Paragraph 2.6.7.1 and 2.6.7.2)

Internal control mechanism in a Government Department is meant to ensure
that its activities are carried out according to the prescribed rules, regulations
and in economical, efficient and effective manner. Strict adherence to statute
codes and manuals minimise the risk of errors and irregularities and help to
protect resources against loss due to waste, abuse and mismanagement.

The WRD is the principal agency lor executing of all irrigation projects in the
State. There are seven major projects, 18 medium projects and 663 minor
ongoing schemes at various stages of construction as of March 2013.

The WRD of the Government of Madhya Pradesh (GOMP) is headed by the
Principal Secretary who is assisted by two Engincers-in-Chief**’ (E-in-C), 11
Chief Engineers (CEs) for overall administration, supervision and control of
works under their zones and headquarters, one Commissioner (Bansagar
Project). one Project Director (MPWSRP), and one Director Command Area
Development. There are 36 Circles headed by Superintending Engineers (SEs)
and 133 divisions headed by Executive Engineers (EEs) for administration,
supervision and control of works under their circles/divisions. Divisional
Accountants (DAs) posted in the divisions, act as financial advisor and internal
auditor of the divisions.

The audit objectives were to assess adequacy and effectiveness 01:

> budgetary and financial controls:

> operational controls related to execution of works; and

Vv

organisational controls related to manpower management for works and
quality control

sponsible for research and designs of projects.

Rule 192 of MPFC provides that the heads of the Departments and other
subordinate authorities are responsible for the submission of correct detailed
estimates punctually on the dates fixed by the Finance Department.

We observed that the Finance Department introduced e-Mudra sotiware for
preparation of budget estimates by the Department. No instance of delay in
submission of Budget was noticed in audit.

Deticiencies in budgetary and expenditure control as well as in accounting,
noticed during audit, have been discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.6.5.2 Expenditure in closing month of the year

According to Rule 174 of MPFC (Volume-l), as far as possible, expenditure
should uniformly be spent throughout the year. Rush of expenditure at the
close of financial year is prone to the risk of excess payment, purchase of
inferior quality goods even at uneconomical rates etc. Thus, the Government
might not get right value for money as the expenditure taking place in such
situation may be without due diligence and care. As per Rule 92 of Budget
Manual, rush of expenditure particularly in the closing months of the financial
year will ordinarily be regarded as breach of financial regularity.

We noticed that the expenditure in the month of March during the years
2010-11 to 2012-13 ranged between 23 and 36 per cent.

We further observed that in respect of seven schemes, expenditure in the month
of March alone ranged from 75 per cent to 100 per cent during the years 2010-
11 and 2011-12. However, during the year 2012-13 position improved, when
expenditure under three schemes during the month of March ranged between
37 and 46 per cent, as shown in table below:

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary, WRD stated
that funds relating to Bundelkhand Package were received in the month of
March; hence there was more expenditure in that month. He further stated that
the nature of work in WRD was seasonal, in rainy season/crop season no work
could be done and it was a normal phenomenon in WRD that expenditure in
first and last quarter of a year accounted for about 60 to 65 per cent of total
expenditure. He further stated that payments for land acquisition were
generally made in closing month to ensure smooth progress.

The reply is not in order because for Bundelkhand package, € 348.06 crore was
released during 2010-11, of which % 154.81 crore was released between August
and October 2010 and the unspent balance of % 193.25 crore was carried
forward for the year 2011-12. Besides, expenditure was proportionately higher
even under the Heads, repair of LISs, restructuring of minor schemes, Repair.
Renovation and Restoration (RRR) etc. as mentioned in the table above where
acquisition of land was not involved.

2.6.5.3 Parking of funds under Civil Deposits

As per Rule 284 of MPTC, no moncy shall be drawn from the treasury unless it
is required for immediate disbursement. It is a serious irregularity to draw
advances from the treasury in anticipation of demands or to prevent lapse of
budget grants. The Civil Deposit account in the Government accounting has
been created with the limited purpose of keeping certain receipts and payments
held in suspense or deposit pending clearance, by payment or recovery, as the
case may be. in cash or otherwise.

We noticed from scrutiny of grant and expenditure statement of F-in-C,
WRD that the central stance of & 427.43 crore received through State
budget during 2010-11 to 2012-13 (Appendix 2.40) were drawn and credited
to Civil Deposits on 31 March of the respective financial years. Out of these
deposits, € 397.32 crore was utilised in subsequent one to three years, whereas
₹ 5.43 crore drawn in the year 2010-11 and % 24.68 crore drawn in the year
2011-12 was not utilised (July 2013). This indicates that money drawn from

ll


In respect of 64
works an amount
of € 313.12 crore
was spent against
the
administratively
approved cost of
% 205.88 crore.

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

the treasury was not required for immediate disbursement and consequently the
fund was kept idle. This indicated a weak financial control in the Department.

In the exit conference (December 2013), it was stated that out of the fund
relating to Bundelkhand Package kept in K-deposit (Civil Deposit), a part
thereof was spent and some of it would be drawn in coming months. This was
necessary to ensure uninterrupted flow of Central funds for projects under this
package. Keeping Central funds received for a particular project is an
established practice. This ensures liquidity as well as committed availability of
project funds. The K-deposit (Civil Deposit) account was opened and funds
were kept there with due approval of the Finance Department.

The reply is not acceptable as drawal of funds, when not required for
immediate disbursement was in violation of the provisions of MPTC and only
those receipts and payments held in suspense account were to be retained as
Civil Deposits. Besides, the Department had furnished utilisation certificates
to Government of India, even while retaining the amounts under Civil
Deposits.

2.6.5.4 (4) Expenditure in excess of Administrative Approval

Rule 147 of MPFC provides that except in cases covered by any special rules
or orders of Government, no work should be commenced or liability incurred
in connection with it, until AA has been obtained from the authority
appropriate in each case. The Department had also issued instructions from
time to time (April 2010) for keeping expenditure within the allotment and AA.

We noticed on examination of original AAs and actual expenditure in respect
of 178 works in seven'® out of 37 test checked divisions that in respect of 64
works an amount of = 313.12 crore was spent up to March 2013 against the
Administratively Approved cost of % 205.88 crore. The overall excess
expenditure of हैं 107.24 crore incurred on these works over the AAs ranged
from 10 per cent to 697 per cent. We further noticed that in respect of the
works of Bargha Nala Tank in Sabalgarh division, an expenditure incurred was
excess by 697 per cent. Besides, in respect of the work of Salipura Tank in
Khargaon Division, expenditure incurred was excess by 673 per cent. Revised
AA for regularising excess expenditure of % 23.29 crore in respect of 16 works
only was obtained during November 2012 to February 2013 after being pointed
out by Audit. However revised AAs regularising the excess expenditure of
& 83.95 crore in respect of remaining 48 works was yet to be obtained as of
March 2013 (Appendix 2.41) though the final bills in 12 works was
finalized/paid.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary, WRD stated
that excess expenditure over AA was due to change in drawings and designs
of the work and price escalation and the matter was under investigation in
respect of remaining works. He also stated that since April 2013 system has
been put in place to restrict drawal of fund to cost plus 10 per cent of AA to
rule out situation of excess expenditure.

Chapter-Il Performance Audit

(B) Purchases without resorting to tendering process

Rule 14 of Store Purchase Rules provides that items enlisted in Annexure ‘B’
of the Rules should only be procured through Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog
Nigam (MPLUN) Limited. If MPLUN is unable to supply the material, the
Department can procure it through open tenders, after obtaining a non-
availability certificate from MPLUN. The Rules further provide that certain
specified items (relating to stationary) can be purchased from Madhya Pradesh
State Consumer Corporation Federation (MPSCCF) without inviting tender.

We noticed in four'® out of 37 test checked divisions that items such as digital

theodolite, electronic total station (Survey equipments) etc. valued at & 2.42
crore enlisted for procurement through MPLUN and not among the specified
items to be purchased from MPSCCF, were purchased by the divisions with
the approval of CE and SE. during the period March 2008 to February 2011
without obtaining non-availability certificates from MPLUN and without
resorting to tendering process. Thus, the purchases were irregular indicating
weak control in sanctions for purchase.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary, WRD stated
that there were only few such cases of purchase in the past and the practice
had been stopped. It was also stated that such cases would be examined and
corrective action taken, where necessary.

reconciliation statements (Form 51) of cheques and remittance as per the

to the Accountant General (A&E) by the divisions.

We noticed that reconciliation statements were not being prepared and sent in
time to the Accountant General (A&E) office by the test checked divisions
excepting four divisions'®. The delays in submission of Form 51 ranged from
1 month to 157 months. In 10 divisions'®, delays in submission of
reconciliation statement were more than five years.

and of हैं 45.76 crore in payments of cheques compared to those in bank
statements which were not reconciled for periods ranging from | month 10155
months as of March 2013 as reconciliation statements were not prepared and
submitted by Divisions for that period. No action for reconciliation of these
differences was taken by the Department indicating weak financial control.

2.6.5.6 Clearance of Miscellaneous Work Advances

According to Paragraph 13.4.1 of CPWD code, Miscellaneous Works
Advance (MWA) is a suspense head of account intended to record transactions
on account of (i) sales on credit, (ii) expenditure incurred on Deposit Works in
excess of deposits received and (iii) losses, retrenchments, errors etc. and other
items of expenditure of allocation which is not known or which cannot be
adjusted until recovery or settlement is effected or write off is ordered. Items
in MWA are cleared either by actual recovery or by transfer under proper
sanction or authority to some other heads of account. The Divisional Officers
are responsible for prompt clearance of the suspense head by recovery or
transfer to the proper heads of accounts concerned.

We noticed that at the end of March 2013, ₹ 17.85 crore against 3,044 items
(transactions) as detailed in Appendix 2.42 were outstanding in 31 out of 37
test checked divisions'® against officials, contractors, suppliers and other
Departments, etc. The oldest pending was trom 1957. There was no systematic
effort for adjustment of the related transactions so as to clear balances in MWA
account and determine the amount payable or recoverable, if any.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary stated that
Miscellaneous Public Works Advance (MPWA) against employees would be
recovered and further efforts would be made to clear the suspense balances.

2.6.5.7 Irregular booking of advance payments for acquisition of land to the
(final head of account

According to Paragraph 10.5.21 of CPWA Code, advance payments made by
the Divisional Officers to LAO for acquisition of land and acquisition charges
is required to be debited to the suspense account “Land Acquisition” in the
accounts of works till the possession of land is received or intimation of actual
payment to the owners of land. This suspense head has to be cleared by the
Divisional Officers on receipt of the accounts along with the statement of
awards of land, vouchers etc. from LAO, by debit to the final head
“Acquisition of Land” in the accounts of works in terms of the para 8.1.12 of
the Code. The creation of suspense account is for the purpose of control over
expenditure and watching receipt of accounts lor actual utilisation of funds
made available to LAO.

2.6.5.8 Credit of lapsed deposit to Government account

According to paragraph 15.4 of CPWA Code, balances above हैं 25 remaining
unclaimed by contractors for more than three complete accounting years,
should be credited to the Government revenue accounts in March each year as
lapsed deposi

We noticed in scrutiny of deposit registers in 20 of the 37 test checked
divisions that deposits of € 16.15 crore remained unclaimed for more than
three years as on 31 March 2013 (Appendix 2.44). The divisions however did
not take action to deposit the amount to the Government account. This
indicated absence of regular review of the deposits with the Department. As a
result, the unclaimed amount could not be accounted for as revenue of the
Government.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary stated that this
was attributed to non-finalisation of bills of contracts in many cases and
informed that many such cases had been cleared. He also stated that unclaimed
deposit would be treated as lapsed after issue of advertisement and notices to
the depositors.

2.6.5.9 Irregular grant of tour advance from works budget

As per Rule 275 of MPFC, the advances for tour are to be drawn and
accounted for as travelling expenses or contingent charges of establishments.
Thus, advance for these purposes should not be drawn from work budget.

We noticed from scrutiny of works cash book in 24 out of 37 test checked
divisions that tour advances aggregating ~ 22.40 lakh were paid to 964
officials during July 2009 to February 2013 by drawing self cheque out of
works budget. These advances remained unadjusted'®’ (March 2013) as no
control register was maintained for watching adjustment of advances so paid.
Unadjusted amounts were significant in Keoti Canal Division, Rewa (® 4.32

No payment was involved in other six divisions which are WR Harsi Dn Dabra, WR
Dn Jaura, HEM Dn Bhopal, HEM Dn Sagar, WR Dn Morena and WR Dn Seoni

2.6.5.10 Unauthorised retention of workers welfare cess

The MP Building & Other Construction Labour Welfare Act, 1996, enacted
with a view to accelerate progress of social security for the unorganised
labours of construction activities, provides for deduction of welfare cess trom
contractors’ bills. According to the Act, one per cent as welfare cess should be
deducted from the amount of contractors’ bills and remitted to the Madhya
Pradesh Building and other Construction Workers’ Welfare Board, constituted
for this purpose within 30 days from deduction. In case of failure, it may
attract penalty at the rate of two per cent tor delay of every month or part of a
month from the date on which such payment is due till such amount is actually
paid. Finance Department, GOMP had also issued instructions in November
2003 for adherence to the provisions of the Act.

We observed in 17 out of 37 test checked divisions that welfare cess
amounting to हैं 5.90 crore was deducted during the period March 2005 to
February 2013 from the contractors’ bills. The amounts were, however, not
remitted to the Board within the specified time. The delays ranged from one to
57 months (Appendix 2.45). The amount of cess was kept as deposited under
Civil Deposits. Thus, the Department has become liable to pay penalty on
account of non-deposit/delay in deposit of the cess indicating weak control
over the function of collection and deposit of the cess.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary stated that
henceforth a system of online deposit of the amount of wellare cess would be
developed and implemented and stated that this would be incorporated in
management information system to monitor deposit of the cess and avoid
delay in deposit.

Operational control systems are designed to ensure that day-to-day actions are
consistent with established plans and objectives. Operational control systems
are derived from the requirements of the management control system.
Deficiencies in operational controls, such as non-maintenance of cash book,
non-submission of material at site (MAS) accounts, non-review of tools and
plant (T&P) register, non-maintenance of contractors ledger and works
abstracts, inadequate inspection of field offices etc. were noticed, which have
been discussed in detail in succeeding paragraphs.

2.6.6.1 Maintenance of contractor’s ledger and works abstract

Contractors’ Ledgers are required to be maintained in terms of Paragraph
10.7.1 of CPWA Code. The form of the contractors’ ledger provides for the
particulars of name of work, bills or voucher, advance payments, secured
advances other transactions and total value of work or supplies. Para 10.5.1 of

116,


Chapter-Il Performance Audit

CPWA Code provides for preparation of works abstracts showing transactions
relating to a single work.

We noticed that contractors’ ledgers and work abstracts were not maintained
in 17 divisions'® and 29 divisions’, respectively. In the absence of such
control records, correctness of transactions relating to advance payments,
secured advances, adjustment of advances and value of work done etc. could
not be checked in divisions as well as by Audit.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary stated that
Contractors’ ledger and Works Abstract would be maintained by linking to
e-measurement of works.

2.6.6.2 Weak control in refund of security deposit obtained from contractors

According to Paragraph 15.5.2 of CPWA Code, before effecting refund of
security deposits received from contractors, the original realisation of deposi
should be traced and a reference to the repayment should be recorded against
the original entry in the cash book and other accounts so as to ensure that
double payments are not made. A certificate for such a note having been made
against the original entry in the cash book and other accounts should be
recorded on all vouchers of refunds.

We noticed in four'”’ out of 37 test checked divisions that security deposits
(SD) amounting to हैं 1.15 crore were refunded to the contractors through hand
receipts during January 2010 to November 2012 without reference to the
original entry in refund vouchers. As a result, Audit could not verity whether
correctness of those refunds was ensured by the divisions. In the absence of
such reference, original entry in the cash book could not be traced in audit.

In the exit conference (December 2013), the Principal Secretary stated that
instructions would be issued for mentioning details against security refunds to
the contractors and for maintaining proper security register.

2.6.6.3 Maintenance of establishment cash book

Paragraph 6.6.1 and 6.6.2 of CPWA Code provides for maintenance of cash
book in the divisions. The amount of cheques drawn on account of the bills for
pay and allowances of the staff should be accounted for in the establishment
cash book.

We noticed in five’! out of 37 test checked divisions that expenditure of

2.6.6.5 Inspections of field offices

inspect respectively the Divisions and Sub-Divisions once in four years.
Further, paragraph 4.114 of the Manual provides that the results of the
inspection of Circles/Divisions/Sub-Divisions should be recorded in the
prescribed forms for taking corrective action.

We noticed from the details given by the divisional offices that during
2010-11 to 2012-13, 139 sub-divisions (out of 583 sub-divisions) were

absence of such records, the nature of irregularities/lapses observed by the

An organisation is required to be established in such a way as to have a control
system tailored to achieve its goals and utilisation of resources in most
efficient and economic manner. Deficiencies in organisational control, such as
inadequate number of laboratories and shortage of staff for quality control
function were noticed, as discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

2.6.7.1 Inadequate number of laboratories

Para 6.016 of MPWD Manual provides that each major/medium project will
have a main laboratory under Research Officer/Assistant Research Officer for
carrying out the tests specified in the Manual. In addition, field laboratories
shall be established at work sites which will be part of the main laboratory to
conduct daily routine tests. There were seven major and 18 medium projects
ongoing as of March 2013. Accordingly, at least 25 main laboratories were
required to be established.

We noticed that only two main laboratories (at Bhopal and Jabalpur) were
established instead of at cach major and medium project. Similarly, field
laboratorics were also not established at any of the work sites. Thus, there was
inadequate establishment for quality control functions. We observed instances
of irregular utilisation of untested material, non-conduct of mandatory tests
and irregular conduction of tests through un-authoirsed/private laboratory
(WR Division, Dhar).

In the exit conference (December 2013), E-in-C stated that the system of
quality control was effective, as he himself and CE’s/SE’s were constantly
monitoring progress of work and quality both. The contract provided for
establishment of laboratory by contractors so as to minimise employees and
maintenance cost.

The fact remains that the establishment of laboratories was not as per the
provisions of the Works Department Manual.

2.6.7.2 Settlement of Audit Inspection Reports

According to Paragraphs 14 and 15 of Appendix 4.15 of MPWD Manual, the
disposal of Audit Inspection Report (IRs) issued by the office of the
Accountant General should be watched in divisions through a control register
and the IRs dealt with in the divisional offices are required to be replied to
Audit Office within a month from the date of their receipt.

of test checked 37 divisions. We also observed from the records of our office
that 1,590 paragraphs of 368 IRs were outstanding in respect of these divisions
as of March 2013. In five divisions'”, more than 20 IRs (74 to 87 paragraphs)
were outstanding. In the absence of the said control register, monitoring and
control over the progress of the settlement of audit paragraphs by higher
authorities and initiative/remedial action on the lapses/irregularities pointed

during 2012-13.

Compliance audit of the Government Departments, their field formations
brought out several instances of lapses in management of resources and

These have been presented in the succeeding paragraphs under broad

For sound financial administration and financial control. it is essential that
expenditure confirms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the
competent authority. This not only prevents irregularities, misappropriations
and frauds, bul helps in maintaining good financial discipline. Some of the
audil (indings on non-compliance with rules and regulations are as under:

(NPV), resulting in short realisation of NPV amounting to हैं 99.81 lakh.

The Government of Madhya Pradesh, Forest Department, (GOMP). issued
(December 2003) directions to recover the amount of NPV", provisionally
after obtaining undertaking from the user agency, at the minimum rate of
& 5.80 lakh per hectare, till further orders. The GOMP in view of orders
(March and May 2008) of the Hon’ble Supreme Court. prescribed
(September 2008) rates for calculating NPV to be charged from user
agencies for diversion of forest land for non-forestry use.

Forest Department did not raise demand for revised Net Present Value

forest ranging from हैं 4.38 lakh to हैं 10.43 lakh per hectare of forest land.

Due to exclusion of expenditure of हैं 6.70 crore incurred on
regeneration for arriving at net profit, an excess payment of dividend
amounting to = 53.58 lakh was distributed to Joint Forest Management
Committees (JFMCs).

Ministry of Environment and Forest. Government of India directed (June
1990) that tribal and other villagers living in and around the forest has first
right on the forest produce. In consonance with this order, Government of
Madhya Pradesh (GOMP) notified (October 2001) ils resolution to distribute
dividend on net profit received from forest produce which would, as per
directions of Principal Chief Conservator of Forest, Madhya Pradesh (May
2008), be expended by JFMCs at their discretion in forest areas and on their
social upliftment. According to the instructions (February 2005) of the
GOMP. ‘Expenditure on Regeneration’ incurred by the Forest Department is
to be deducted from receipts from sale of timber and bamboo to arrive at the
‘Net Profit’ for the purpose of calculation of dividend®. which is distributed
to JFMCs. As per rule 136 (8) 54 of Madhya Pradesh Forest Financial
Rules-1979. expenditure on regeneration includes expenditure incurred on
sowing of seeds, plants, digging of pits. changing of soil, fertilizers,
transportation, plantation, pesticides, weeding and security.

During scrutiny of records of two Divisional Forest Officers (DFO) al
Dindori and East Mandla, we observed (August 2012 and January 2013)
that a total amount of % 5.44 crore was distributed during 2010-11 and 2011-
12 among 92 JFMCs of these two Divisions on the basis of the net profit
(® 68.02 crore). We observed that while calculating the dividend,

distributed to IFMCs.

In earthwork of canal banks, watering and compaction were not

Further, the Unified Schedule of Rates (USR) for works provides that if the

The work of “construction of balance earthwork of Mahan main canal”,
Sidhi from RD" km 0.10 to RD km 22.50 and its minors and structures was
awarded to a contractor in October 2010 for % 7.26 crore. The agreement
provided for execution of earthwork for canal banks including watering and
compaction in accordance with the irrigation specifications. The work
stipulated for completion by April 2011 was completed by the due date and
final bill was paid to the contractor for € 6.19 crore as of July 2012

The estimates. inter alia. provided a clubbed rate of € 112.46 per cu m for
the item ‘Earthwork for construction of canals with approved soil including
watering and compaction’. According to the clubbing statement (rate
analysis) prepared by the Department, the rate for the said item included
elements of € 12 per cu m and & 13 per cu m towards watering and
compaction, respectively. Against this, the contractor quoted हैं 70 per cum
for the clubbed item.

We noticed (December 2012) that the contractor executed 1,55,000.75
cu m'! earthwork but as per Measurement Book” the work of watering and
compaction in earthwork was not executed by the contractor. After
deduction for shrinkage at the rate of 20 per cent in terms of USR, to arrive
at the net quantity of earthwork, the contractor was paid for 1,24,000.60
cum, at his quoted rate of 70 per cu m for earthwork (value: % 86.80 lakh)
Since watering and compaction was not done by the contractor. a further
deduction of € 15,567 per cu m was also required to be made which was not
deducted from contractor's bill. This resulted in the excess payment of
₹ 19.29 lakh (1,24,000 cu m x हैं 15.56) to the contractor.

In the same reaches of the canal, heavy seepage was noticed by the

On this being pointed out in audit (December 2012), the Executive Engineer
(EE) Mahan Canal Division Sidhi stated that there was no provision made in
USR to deduct the proportionate cost of watering and compaction.

The reply of EE is not correct as the USR provides rates for items of
watering and compaction separately. The rate of € 70 per cu m was quoted
for clubbed items including watering and compaction. Since the contractor
did not execute watering and compaction. the value of items not executed
should have been deducted while making payment to the contractor.
Besides, in the similar case’’ the Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C), Water
Resources Department (WRD), accepted (October 2012) the audit
observations.

Further, to stop seepage in the canal, the Chief Engineer, Ganga Basin,
Rewa accorded technical sanction (July 2011) for Cement Concrete (CC)
lining in the canal for हैं 31.50 crore. According to the technical circular
(January 1984) of F-in-C, WRD for CC lining, earthwork should be watered
and compacted irrespective of embankment height. Since the earthwork was
neither watered nor compacted, CC lining work was at risk of deterioration
due to unstable base.

The matter was referred to the Government (June, August 2013 and January
2014): their reply has not been received (January 2014).

Authorisation of expenditure from public fund is to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered lo incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance
as a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own
money and should enforce financial order and strict economy at every step.
Audit has detected instances of impropriety, extra and infructuous
expenditure. some of which are mentioned below

WATER RESOURCES DEPARTMENT

The Department executed 1,17,517 cu m of rock toe, stone pitching etc.
at full rate instead of labour rate only by ignoring 1.44 lakh cu m of
hard rock available from earth excavation. This resulted in avoidable
extra expenditure of f 1.22 crore.

As per para 4.13.5.2 and 4.1.4 of Specifications for Irrigation Projects
(Volume-1), a large amount of stones/spalls required for construction of dry
stone pitching and rock toe should be obtained from excavation of other
parts of work. Additional rock as required should be obtained from rock
quarries. The Unified Schedule of Rates (USR) further provides that the
excavated hard rock should be utilised in the same work for which only
labour rates shall be payable.

The Water Resources Department (WRD) awarded (May 2008 to February
2011) the work of construction of four minor irrigation tank under four
Divisions! to contractors at a cost of € 34.84 crore for completion between
November 2009 and July 2012. Two tanks” were completed in October
2011 and March 2013 and the work on two other was in progress as of
December 2013. Payments were made to contractors between December
2011 and December 2013.

During scrutiny of records of the four Divisions we noticed (October 2012 to
January 2013) that estimates of the four tanks provided for total excavation
of 1,30,117.02 cu m hard rock from cul off trench (COT), sluice and
spill/approach channel and execution of 1.25.170.02 cu m rock toe, stone
pitching in the dam/tank. However, for execution of rock toe, stone pitching
in the dam/tank the rate was fixed as complete item considering carting of
stone/boulders from borrow area with a lead of 2 km to 10 km including cost
of material. Thus, the hard rock available from excavation from COT, sluice
etc. was not considered for determining the rate for the work of execution of
rock toe, stone pitching in the dam/tank. The contractors excavated
(December 2011 to March 2013) 1,44,004.235 cu m hard rock (as per

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year after year, it
becomes pervasive, when it is prevailing in the entire system. Recurrence of
irregularities despite being pointed out in earlier audits. is not only
indicative of non-seriousness on the part of the Executive but is also an
indication of lack of effective monitoring. This, in turn, encourages willful
deviations from observance of rules/regulations and results in weakness of

Adoption of incorrect parameters in computation of traffic intensity,

As per para 2.028 of Works Department manual, an officer according the
technical sanction to an estimate is responsible for soundness of design
According to the Indian Road Congress (IRC:37*) specifications, the crust
(thickness of pavement) as well as type of Bituminous Course is designed on
the basis of design traffic in terms of Million Standard Axle (४५.५)? and
Califomia Bearing Ratio (087) of sub-grade*'. Wherever the designed
traffic is less than one MSA and CBR of sub-grade is up to 10 per cent,
provision of only 20 mm OGPC with seal coat should be provided as a
bituminous wearing course and there is no need of BM.

ignored the provisions of IRC-37 for ascertaining crust for preparation of
estimates and yranting the TS and adopted richer specification of bituminous
base course consisting of BM/dense bituminous macadam (DBM) and
SDBC as detailed below:

of & 64.65 crore for completion in 24 months i.e. by October 2011. The
work was in progress as of December 2012 and up to the 490 running
account bill 72.68 crore (including escalation of हैं 7.72 crore) was paid to
the contractor in December 2013.

tudit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

Para 2.40.1 of standard agreement of Works Department (WD) manual
provides formula for calculating component-wise escalation but ratio of the
components (labour. material and POL”) in total value of work is not given.
The Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C) issued (January 1985) instructions for
incorporating in Notice Inviting Tender (NIT) the components of labour.
material and POL as 40, 20 and 7.5 per cent respectively of total value of
work done for the work of earthen dam.

We noticed (December 2012 and January 2014) that at the time of floating
the tenders (September 2008), the price escalation clause 2.40.1 was made
"Not applicable" in the tender document though escalation was payable since
the completion schedule was more than 18 months. The space provided in
the tender, (subsequently formed as agreement) for fixing the price
adjustment component for labour, material and POL was left blank. The last
date of receipt of tender was 16 October 2008. Meanwhile, a corrigendum
was issued (8 October 2008) by EE at the instance of the Chief Engineer
(CE) making the escalation clause (2.40.1) applicable for the work, but
without specitving the ratio of the components (labour, material and POL) in
terms of value of work. We observed that the standard agreement form
contains a footnote for filling the blanks in accordance with E-in-C’s
circular (January 1985), 1.0. 40, 20 and 7.5 per cent respectively. However,
after issue of the corrigendum EE adopted (October 2009) 10, 60 and 30 per
cent for labour, material and POL respectively without approval of E-in-C.
Due to adoption of higher percentages for two components with lower
percentage for the other, for the purpose of price adjustment, undue financial
benelit of हैं 2.46 crore was extended to the contractor as detailed in table
below:

Table 3.2: Excess payment to the contractor on account of escalation

The Government stated (September 2013) that the tender document was

accordingly

adopted the ratio of three components for escalation ignoring the orders of
1985 which was also referred in NIT. Therefore, the payment was to be
regulated in accordance with the circular as clarified in the agreement.

Chapter IIT: Audit of Transactions

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the
people through fulfillment of certain goals in the area of health, education,
development and upgradation of infrastructure and public service. However,
audit scrutiny revealed instances where in the funds released by the
Government for creating public assets for the benefit of the community
remained unutilised/blocked and/or proved unfruitful/unproductive due to
indecisiveness. lack of administrative oversight and concerted action at
various levels. A few such cases have been discussed below

NARMADA VALLEY DEVELOPMENT DEPARTME

Overall survival rate of plants in compensatory afforestation remained
below 30 per cent against the specified target of 75 per cent due to
improper planning and execution. This resulted in infructuous
expenditure of f 1.20 crore.

In view of the orders of the Hon’ble Supreme Court (September 2000), the
Chief Conservator of Forest (Land Survey) Madhya Pradesh (CCF) issued
instructions (February 2004) to achieve minimum survival percentage of
plants in compensatory afforestation at 75 per cent, well above the survival
percentage™ specified in the Government orders of October 1986 and
prepare the technical and financial planning including provision for
iuigalion, fertilizers and other treatments in the project. Further
instructions issued (June 2004) by CCF, copy of which was endorsed to
Narmada Valley Development Authority (NVDA), stated that the survival
percentage shall be applicable to all plantations of compensatory
afforestation undertaken after December 2001

During scrutiny of records* of two Divisional Forest Officers** (DFOs),
we observed (December 2012 and March 2013) that in 12
project/plantation sites Compensatory Afforestation was initiated in
2003-04 and 2004-05 with a project period of seven years to be completed
between 2009-10 and 2010-11. In three project sites of Icchawar Division
project was prepared for six years but the implementation period of
maintenance was further reduced. Out of these 12 projects, nine projects
were handed over during the period March to June 2010 and three projects
were handed over during the period April to May 2013 to regular territorial
divisions of the Forest Department. Total expenditure incurred on the
projects was हैं 2.02 crore.

Between 20 per cent and 40 per cent depending upon the regions of the State.
Compartment histories, project reports, plantation journal, expenditure statements, and
monitoring reports

DEO (Compensatory Afforestation) Ichhawar, Bhopal and DFO (Compensatory
Afforestation) Kaveri, Khandwa of NVDA. the DFO's report to Member
(Environment and Forest) NVDA.

137


Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2013

We observed that, out of 6,55,115 saplings planted during July/August
2004 to July 2005 in 12 projects, only 1.94,208 (overall 30 per cent)
survived al the time of handing over i.e. al the end of the respective project
period. The survival rate in the projects ranged between 20 per cent and 50
per cent, (Appendix 3.12) which was well below the stipulated survival of
75 per cent. The low survival rate was attributable to the following:

> As per the project reports of plantation, the projects did not make
provisions for irrigation of the saplings planted

> The projects also provided for other treatments like weeding only in the
first year of maintenance and not in all the years of maintenance (310
7 years), although mortality occurred in each year after plantation,
year-wise details of mortality and total expenditure are given in
Appendix 3.11.

Thus, lack of irrigation facilities and lack of maintenance and other
treatments like weeding resulted in the low survival of plants. Considering
the loss factor of 0.33 to 0.73, the infructuous expenditure work out to
= 1.20 crore as detailed in Appendix 3.12.

On this being pointed out, the Government in their reply stated (August
2013) that 75 per cent survival was possible only in irrigated plantation
and stated that project for these plantations were approved prior to CCF’s
direction (February 2004) and hence could not be applied retrospectively
The Government further stated that irrigation facilities could not be
provided due to absence of perennial water source on the sites and
accordingly, plantation project for un-irrigated plantation was prepared and
executed in two districts Khargone and Sehore. These were areas of
deficient rainfall, high temperature, high biolic pressure and low sile
quality. Besides, Sehore and Khargone received scanty rainfall and high
temperature after plantation in the year 2004 and 2005 respectively,
affecting survival adversely. The Government further stated that in view of
norms set for survival percentage of un-irrigated plantations by Forest
Department (October 1986), these plantations were successful

The reply of the Government is not correct as the instructions of CCF
(February 2004) based on Hon’ble Supreme Court orders (September
2000) regarding compensatory afforestation were issued well before the
start of plantation projects (2004-05). Besides, in view of the climatic
conditions of the sites selected, necessary provisions for irrigation and
other suitable facilities should have been made accordingly.

PUBLIC WORKS DEPART!

Starting of work without obtaining approval of Forest Department led
to stoppage of upgradation work of Unchehra-Parasmaniya-Dureha
road midway. Late initiation for forest clearance and re-tendering of
the balance work resulted in increase in cost amounting to = 2.39
crore.

According to the orders issued (June 2000) by the Government of Madhya
Pradesh (GOMP). it is necessary to obtain permission from the Forest
Department before taking up work of upgradation of roads in forest areas.
The detailed instructions for granting approval for the upgradation of such
roads were issued (May 2005) by the Forest Depariment and the copy of
the same was endorsed to the Principal Secretary, Public Works
Department (PWD).

Scrutiny of PWD, B&R Division, Satna revealed (March 2013) that the
work for upgradation of the existing 43 km Unchehra-Parasmaniya-Dureha
road was awarded (January 2008) to a contractor at an estimated cost of
& 8.53 crore. The work was scheduled for completion in 16 months i.e. by
May 2009, The estimate was revised (September 2008) to हैं 10.78 crore on
the ground of cost escalation and further revised (August 2010) to हैं 11.30
crore on the ground of site specific modifications”.

The contractor completed (January 2009) the work in 25.20 km length
But. the work in the remaining 17.80 km length could not be completed as
the Forest Department stopped (January 2009) the work due to undertaking
the work without obtaining prior permission from it. On the ground of
delay and increase in cost of material, the contractor requested (April
2012) the Department to foreclose the work under clause 147 of the
agreement. Based on orders of the Enygineer-in-Chief (E-in-C), the
Execulive Engineer (EE), PWD (B&R) Division, Satna foreclosed (May
2012) the work. The final bill was paid (June 2012) to the contractor for
total value of work done amounting to % 7.18 crore.

We noticed (March 2013) that though 35 km road out of the total road
length (43 km) was passing through forest ज्ञात", the Chief Engineer (CE),
PWD Rewa Zone, Rewa accorded (August 2007) technical sanction of the
work without obtaining permission of the Forest Department. The EF
submitted proposal to Divisional Forest Officer (DFO), Satna, only in
April 2009 seeking permission to take up the balance work of upgradation
of the road. which was obtained in January 2012.

Provision of 65 Hume Pipe culverts and 4 medium bridges as well as provision of wet
mixed macadam in 25.80 km length of road

WATER RESOURCES DEPARTMENT

‘Providing LDPE film below canal lining’ was included in the estimate
of a canal work, but not got executed. This resulted in vitiating of
tendering process because the lowest tenderer would no longer have
been the lowest, and the cost had become lower by रैं 68.13 lakh if the
item of LDPE film, for which lowest tenderer had quoted only ₹ै 1 per
sq m against the estimate of 22 per sq m, was excluded from the
estimates before entering into tendering process.

As per para 2.028 of Works Department Manual, an officer according the
technical sanction to an estimate is responsible for soundness of design and
for incorporating the required items for inclusion in the estimates. Further,
according to the technical circular issued (1984) by the Engineer-in-Chief
(E-in-C), black Low Density Polyethylene (LDPE) film should be used
below the base mortar of plain cement concrete and flag stone lining and
concrete cas/-in-situ slabs. It may be avoided in non-water absorbent sub-
grade.

Scrutiny of records (February 2013) of Harsi High Level Canal Division,
Dabra (Gwalior) and further information collected (January 2014) revealed
